Stochastic Modelling of Elasticity Tensor Fields
Sharana Kumar Shivanand, Bojana Rosić, Hermann G. Matthies

Abstract
We present a novel framework for the probabilistic modelling of random fourth order material tensor fields, with a focus on tensors that are physically symmetric and positive definite (SPD), of which the elasticity tensor is a prime example. Given the critical role that spatial symmetries and invariances play in determining material behaviour, it is essential to incorporate these aspects into the probabilistic description and modelling of material properties. In particular, we focus on spatial point symmetries or invariances under rotations, a classical subject in elasticity. Following this, we formulate a stochastic modelling framework using a Lie algebra representation via a memoryless transformation that respects the requirements of positive definiteness and invariance. With this, it is shown how to generate a random ensemble of elasticity tensors that allows an independent control of strength, eigenstrain, and orientation. The procedure also accommodates the requirement to prescribe specific spatial symmetries and invariances for each member of the whole ensemble, while ensuring that the mean or expected value of the ensemble conforms to a potentially ‘higher’ class of spatial invariance. Furthermore, it is important to highlight that the set of SPD tensors forms a differentiable manifold, which geometrically corresponds to an open cone within the ambient space of symmetric tensors. Thus, we explore the mathematical structure of the underlying sample space of such tensors, and introduce a new distance measure or metric, called the ‘elasticity metric’ , between the tensors. Finally, we model and visualize a one-dimensional spatial field of orthotropic Kelvin matrices using interpolation based on the elasticity metric.



Keywords:

stochastic material modelling, random elasticity, tensor-valued random field,
spatial symmetries of ensemble and mean, Fréchet mean, Lie algebra representation,
directional and scaling uncertainty, uncertainty quantification

1
Introduction

The computational modelling of heterogeneous materials often has to resort to a
statistical or probabilistic description, as the precise details are unknown or uncertain.
The material properties are usually collected in tensor quantities,
and these tensors are often of even order, and due to Onsager’s reciprocity relations

[
38
]
physically symmetric — in elasticity this is termed the
major

symmetry — and additionally they are frequently positive definite as they are associated
with e.g. stored energy or entropy production
[
180
]
. This class of even-order physically
symmetric and positive definite (SPD) tensors — and here especially the important class of
4th-order tensors and their associated symmetry classes — is the main focus in this contribution.
The stochastic modelling procedure we shall describe is applicable to any such even-order physically
symmetric and possibly additionally positive definite tensor, but we shall spell
things out in detail for 4th order SPD tensors, a prime example of which is
the elasticity tensor, which will be taken here to stand for all such tensors.

Well known examples of such SPD tensors are e.g. thermal conductivity —
a 2nd order tensor mapping temperature gradients to thermal fluxes — or, as
already mentioned above, the
elasticity tensor of a linearly elastic material as
a fourth order tensor, mapping strains to stresses.
A 6th order more involved example is the complete piezo-elasticity tensor,
mapping the combination of strains and electric displacement to stress and electric field.
Such a tensor of a coupled problem would be best tackled using the tensor-block
matrices of
[
127
]
, which would be a more or less straightforward extension
of the concepts treated here.

The representation and stochastic modelling of 2nd order tensors, such as those
describing diffusion phenomena, was shown in a precursor paper
[
160
]
.
Here we want to follow along the general lines of this approach based on the spectral decomposition
and symmetry-group reduction, but for higher-order tensors a novel situation arises, namely
the situation that the symmetry-group reduction
[
149
,
46
,
86
]

can not diagonalise the tensor. We outline the general
idea for SPD tensors of any even order but concentrate in detail on 4th-order tensors.

As the interest here is in the probabilistic modelling of such tensors, a simple way
to realise this is to think of the tensor entries as random variables.
But an important aspect of a material law described by such
a tensor is its invariance under spatial symmetries
[
124
,
161
,
179
,
149
,
46
,
86
]
.
Although the numerical values of the tensor entries may be uncertain resp. random variables,
it is not uncommon that the class of spatial symmetries is known exactly, both for
each individual realisation as well as for the mean.
These invariances under spatial symmetries are a classical subject
[
101
]
,
and the different symmetry groups allow one to assign such material laws,
resp. the tensors describing them into certain classes, e.g. like the well-known
isotropic class, or the orthotropic class of materials.
2nd order tensors in 3D show, next to these just mentioned invariance resp. symmetry
classes of iso- or ortho-tropy, additionally only plan-isotropic behaviour
(e.g.
[
101
,
160
]
).
4th order spatial tensors, however, have a richer set of symmetry classes than 2nd order tensors
(e.g.
[
28
,
19
,
101
]
),
namely eight symmetry or invariance classes in 3D
[
51
]
, and four in
2D
[
15
]
, cf. also the general references
[
124
,
161
,
179
]
.

Any even-order tensor may be viewed as a linear map when acting on the space of tensors of half
the order by contracting over half the indices, e.g.
[
118
,
126
,
129
]
.
In the case of the 4th order elasticity tensor, this space of tensors half the order
is the space of strains, i.e. 2nd order symmetric tensors, as the elasticity
tensor maps a strain tensor into the corresponding stress tensor.
By choosing appropriate bases in the vector space of tensors of half the order
[
118
]
,
this linear map can be represented by a matrix; so the class of tensors we are interested in can
thus be represented by symmetric positive definite (SPD) matrices. We shall switch to
this matrix representation whenever it is convenient to do so.
The spatial invariance resp. symmetry groups are then realised as linear transformation
groups on the relevant space of half-order tensors, or, equivalently, on the space underlying
the representing symmetric matrices just mentioned. These transformation groups then define
invariant subspaces of the SPD matrix representing the tensor

[
149
,
46
,
86
]
.
By transforming into a basis adapted to these invariant subspaces, the matrix takes on a
block-diagonal form. This transformation to block-diagonal form is termed the
symmetry resp. invariance group reduction or decomposition.
Here we shall prefer the term ‘invariance’ of the tensor resp. matrix under the spatial
group operations
over the term ‘symmetry’, in order to avoid confusion with the physical symmetry due to
Onsager’s reciprocity relations, which results in symmetric matrices.

Any SPD matrix or linear map can be spectrally decomposed
with positive eigenvalues, and has orthogonal eigenvectors.
This spectral eigenvalue / -vector (EV) decomposition separates the size, strength,
or stiffness information contained in the eigenvalues
from the information about the orientation of the eigenvectors. For the sake of brevity
and simplicity, in the following we shall speak about the eigenvalues in terms
of stiffness or elastic moduli as they apply to the elasticity or Hooke tensor,
but obviously this can be easily translated to any other physical property.
The group reduction together with the spectral
decomposition are the main building blocks
of our approach. In the representation proposed, and in this we see one of the
novelties of our approach, we want to keep the modelling of strength / stiffness,
eigenstrain distribution, and spatial orientation
separate and thus be able to control it independently.
So, finally, one ends up considering
random SPD matrices, where certain invariances are known for the whole population,
as well as for the mean.
There is already extensive work on random matrices, drawing on several sources,
which is mentioned only very briefly here
(e.g.
[
73
,
157
,
162
,
72
]
).

The separation of stiffness, eigenstrains, and spatial orientation information is
actually an old subject, as the
characterisation of elasticity tensors through their eigenvalues resp. moduli
was already started by Stokes (cf.
[
95
,
181
,
127
]
) and
Lord Kelvin (W.K. Thomson)
[
177
,
178
]
.
It was little appreciated at the time and then apparently almost forgotten, to be
picked up again about a century later by
[
47
]
, and subsequently, among others, by

[
153
,
141
,
143
,
142
,
113
,
171
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
180
,
154
,
155
,
16
,
3
,
91
,
127
,
128
,
129
,
130
]
.
Kelvin originally used this to classify material symmetries; a historical account may be
found in
[
75
,
76
]
. It is an early example of a tensor representation
through an orthogonal tensor
decomposition, and many other such representations and decompositions have been considered;
a compilation and comparison of different orthogonal
decompositions may be found in
[
21
,
23
,
22
]
.
Furthermore, topics of tensor representation are also treated in

[
136
,
7
,
99
,
101
]
,
and more information on tensor decompositions may also be found in e.g.
[
11
,
183
,
13
,
12
,
14
,
52
,
188
,
118
,
40
,
119
,
81
,
17
,
137
,
138
,
39
]
.

The representation preferred by us, based on
the spectral decomposition referred to above, offers the possibility of separately
modelling stiffness, orientation, and possibly the eigenstrain, as well as
separately controlling their individual probability distributions.
Relative to existing models this offers a direct and fine
control of those properties, while being free of any limitations.
It additionally offers direct control of the symmetry resp. invariance properties
to be discussed below, both of the ensemble mean and of each individual realisation.
This feature distinguishes our approach from existing models. This and further distinctions are
discussed in more detail in
[
160
]
, and will not be repeated here
.

It is well known that there are eight symmetry or invariance classes
for symmetric 4th order tensors in 3D, they may be found in the
references just quoted, as well as in the reference works
[
124
,
161
,
179
]
.
To sketch the connection of the spectral or EV-decomposition with the idea of invariance under
symmetry groups, it was already mentioned that one considers the representation of the
elasticity tensor as a linear map through a proper choice of a basis in the space of strains.

In the case of the elasticity tensor this is known as the Kelvin notation.

The eigenvalues of the Kelvin matrix are traditionally
called
Kelvin moduli
and the corresponding eigenvectors
Kelvin modes
.

The invariance class of the material or elasticity tensor
is identified by rotating the coordinate system of the physical space
in such a way as to introduce
as many zeros as possible in the tensor.
This pattern then turns out to be stable
under a whole subgroup of rotations, which is the point invariance resp. symmetry group of the
material resp. the tensor or the Kelvin matrix.
This process of
group reduction
is an algorithm to find orthogonal invariant subspaces
which reduce the matrix to block-diagonal form (e.g.
[
149
,
46
]
),
thereby also identifying the invariance resp. symmetry group.
For 4th order tensors resp. Kelvin matrices this group is represented
as a subgroup of the group of orthogonal transformations on strain-space,
cf. e.g.
[
98
,
99
,
100
,
101
]
. It also determines the eigenstrain distribution
in the relevant elasticity class

[
133
,
28
,
31
,
96
,
19
]
.

The group-reduced block-diagonal form alluded to above is the representation
usually chosen in the literature when the different elasticity classes are discussed, e.g.
[
28
,
19
,
101
,
124
,
161
,
179
]
.
The block-diagonal form of the matrix can be further reduced to diagonal
form in each block with a orthogonal transformation to a
direct sum of specific eigenstrain subspaces.
This last orthogonal transformation in strain-space does not result from a
spatial rotation, but is purely in strain-space.
This consideration of iso-spectral eigenstrain distributions is
a crucial extension to higher-order tensors of the analogous development
in
[
160
]
for 2nd order tensors.

The 4th order elasticity tensor may thus be represented in a two-stage procedure
by a combined group reduction and spectral decomposition,
i.e. one may represent the elasticity tensor in form of the
Kelvin matrix by a positive diagonal matrix of eigenvalues resp. Kelvin moduli, an
‘eigenstrain distributor’ rotation within an invariant subspace of the
block-diagonal form, and the spatially induced
rotation for the symmetry group reduction. The task of representing random SPD tensor
fields thus is reduced to representing these three components.
It is worthwhile, and in our context essential, to observe that these three ingredients
— spatially induced orthogonal transformations for the group reduction, general
orthogonal transformation on strain-space to diagonalise each block, and diagonal SPD
matrices of Kelvin moduli — are each members of matrix Lie groups.

In the modelling of random tensor fields, the most comfortable and desirable way is
to find an unconstrained representation on a linear vector space.
For the purpose of modelling or representing a random SPD tensor, a geometric point of
view might be helpful. In the vector space of physically symmetric tensors of a given
even order, the positive definite ones (SPD tensors) form an open convex cone,
i.e. sums of SPD tensors with positive coefficients are again SPD tensors.
They thus form a differentiable manifold, but not a vector space.
So the desire is to represent this cone on a vector space without any constraints.
One such possibility is the matrix exponential and logarithm (e.g.
[
89
,
62
,
61
,
160
]
),
and this can be used in different guises, and will also be used here.
The Lie groups just alluded to — diagonal
SPD matrices and subgroups of orthogonal matrices — which we want to use in the
representation, as the they allow one to independently model different aspects
of the SPD tensor, are geometrically speaking more complicated than the simple vector space
of physically symmetric tensors. But the exponential and logarithm may be used in this
situation as well — here coinciding with the Lie group exponential and logarithm — and
furnish a representation on the associated Lie algebra of the corresponding group, which
is geometrically the tangent space at the group identity; effectively giving a representation
on a direct sum of linear spaces.

In this context it is worthwhile to note that the nature of the set of SPD tensors — a differentiable
manifold which is geometrically an open cone in the ambient space of symmetric tensors — suggests
that the calculation of averages, means, or expected values inherited from this ambient space
based on a Euclidean metric is not the natural one to use. It has actually been observed that
using the mean based on the Euclidean metric has some undesirable properties

[
146
,
2
,
116
,
4
,
5
,
43
,
44
,
184
,
54
,
48
]
, in particular
the so-called swelling, fattening, and shrinking effects

[
157
,
89
,
158
,
62
,
61
,
48
]
.

To wit, when finding the midpoint (average) or interpolating between two points on
earth’s surface, one will usually not mean the midpoint of a straight connecting line
(the average of the ambient Euclidean space), but the intrinsic midpoint half way on a great circle.
This intrinsic kind of average or mean using a non-Euclidean metric is called a
Fréchet
mean,
and will be shortly recapped in Appendix
A
, recalling the connection between
metric and averaging. From a metric, one may define variationally the Fréchet or Karcher
mean — a generalisation of the arithmetic resp. Euclidean distance based mean,
see Appendix
A
— to more general metric spaces
[
125
]
.
This is an area of active research
[
48
,
173
,
174
,
175
,
88
,
64
]
,
and the ‘best’ metric and mean seem to be application dependent.

The effect of using a mean which is not adapted to the manifold can have various consequences.
When modelling a non-isotropic material, this may mean a loss of anisotropy for the mean,
which may be undesirable in some situations. In the above literature — particularly that
coming from the medical field of diffusion tensor imaging, where these undesirable
effects are not acceptable, but also in the estimation of covariance matrices — there
is discussion on what metric to use
[
2
,
116
,
4
,
145
,
119
,
44
,
74
,
48
,
62
,
166
,
147
,
65
,
176
,
88
,
64
]
.
For elasticity tensors, this topic of a metric has also attracted some
attention
[
28
,
117
,
120
,
18
,
54
,
185
,
122
,
169
,
160
]
,
e.g. in finding the closest elasticity tensor of some invariance class to measurement data.

We formulate some desiderata for a mean resp. a metric for the case of elasticity.
This will be connected to the proposed representation on a direct sum of Lie algebras, which
allows one to choose inner products on the Lie algebras to define a metric, which is transported
via the exponential map to the Lie group and hence gives rise to a Riemannian structure
on the group, which can then be used to define
a metric — which we suggest to call the
‘elasticity metric’
— on the cone of
positive definite tensors which satisfies the proposed desiderata;
the details of this are to be found in the Appendix
A
.
Based on this distance one may then formulate the corresponding Fréchet mean,
which may be called the
‘elasticity mean’
.

To generate random SPD matrices, a reduced non-parametric approach taking account
of positive definiteness was presented in
[
162
,
163
]
.
Here the algebraic property that positive elements in the algebra are squares of other
elements in the algebra
[
159
,
186
]
is used to ascertain positive
definiteness. This then forms the basis for the generation of
random elasticity tensors with specified invariance in the mean and fully anisotropic invariance
in each realisation, resp. controlled elasticity class invariance with constant spatial
orientation of the symmetry axes in each realisation, which is shown in a series of papers

[
66
,
67
,
68
,
69
,
70
,
71
,
132
,
168
,
165
]
.

In the area of modelling of random tensor fields with given invariance properties,
the culmination of a long series of works
[
140
,
97
,
98
,
99
,
100
]
is reported in
[
101
]
,
specifically looking at stochastically homogeneous and isotropic random tensor fields
of any order in a three dimensional domain. The spatial modelling uses the spectral theorem for
the covariance function, to generate a spectral or
Karhunen-Loève
like representation
(e.g.
[
108
]
) of the random tensor field with the desired invariance properties.
No particular attention is paid to positive definiteness, as the methods used
are purely vector space like, such as linear combinations or series and integrals.
The danger is here that
although the limiting expression may well represent a positive definite tensor,
in a practical computational situations these series and integrals have to be
truncated resp. numerically approximated, and this step may lead to an unacceptable
numerical loss of positive definiteness
everywhere
.

In some associated numerical examples of anti-plane shear
[
187
]
,
positive definiteness everywhere is obtained by using a deterministic SPD base tensor and adding
a random rank-one tensor — a dyadic or tensor product of identical vectors and thus
automatically positive — and controlling the random coefficient so that the sum stays SPD.

Here we shall also use a Karhunen-Loève like
field model to represent the parameters in the Lie algebras mentioned above,
but by modelling the logarithm of the tensor one can ensure positive
definiteness
everywhere
via the exponential map for each realisation at each point in space.

Ensuring positive definiteness everywhere through the use of the exponential function,
resp. the logarithm in the opposite direction, is an often used approach;
it typically based on the spectral or EV-decomposition. As the spectral decomposition
may be written as a sum of dyadic or tensor products of eigenvectors, this furnishes
a connection to the above mentioned approach in
[
187
]
.
The exponential function
is employed in e.g.
[
70
,
132
,
89
,
158
,
62
,
61
,
160
,
88
]
,
and it may be combined with the squaring
approach
[
70
,
132
,
60
]
to ensure compliance with
a particular invariance class. This log-modelling is quite common
when measuring diffusion and conductivity tensors, i.e. focusing the description
on the logarithm of the tensor. This takes advantage of the fact that the
invariance classes are the same for a tensor and its exponential resp. logarithm.
Also in this work the exponential function is used in an essential way
to ensure positive definiteness numerically in each realisation.
The exponential function may be even used to model the rotations, essentially parametrising
the angles
[
60
]
. Here we follow
[
160
]
and consequently
model the eigenvalues and the rotations via the exponential function, mapping from
a Lie algebra to the corresponding Lie group

The structure of the paper is outlined as follows: Section
2
collects
essential preliminaries,
including the notation for even-order tensors in Section
2.1
and Kelvin’s matrix representation of Hooke’s law in Section
2.2
, with further details provided in Appendix
C
.
Section
2.3
discusses the transformation of the Kelvin matrix under rotations and briefly addresses how spatial rotations are modelled in the sequel,
and how they influence the strain-space. Additional background on rotations can be found in
Appendix
B
.
This section also introduces the reduction of the Kelvin matrix to block diagonal form, its spectral decomposition, and the formulation of group invariance in elasticity. In order to make this work more easily accessible, the group theoretic material
will be kept to a minimum.
The representation and modelling of fourth-order SPD tensor fields, based on fields of parameters, is detailed in Section
2.4
.

The main part of the paper is Section
3
, where Section
3.1
covers the Lie representation of the Kelvin matrix, and Section
3.2
introduces the concept of a Fréchet mean for SPD tensors. This uses the proposed ‘elasticity metric’
which is described in Appendix
A
.
In Section
3.3
, we formulate our requirements for a representation
of SPD tensors, as well as some general thoughts and desiderata on how to average
SPD tensors based on Fréchet means.
Next, in Section
3.4
, we introduce the framework for generating random ensembles of Kelvin matrices at a spatial point, followed by a description of spatial variation. The focus here is on modelling randomness through the separation of stiffness, eigenstrains, and spatial orientation.
Section
4

explores Kelvin’s spectral decomposition of Hooke’s law in detail, with the relationship to 2D and 3D elasticity classes covered in
Section
4.1
and Section
4.2
, respectively. Finally, the paper concludes in Section
5

2
Preliminaries

We consider physical space as given by

\mathcal{V}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the physically interesting instances being the 2D situation
with
d=2
, and the full 3D case with
d=3
. For the sake of simplicity we shall
consider only Cartesian coordinate systems and orthonormal bases,
so we do not distinguish co- and contra-variant indices.

2.1
Even order tensors as linear maps

Vectors in
\mathcal{V}
like the position vector are denoted as
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}=(x_{i})\in\mathcal{V}
,
whereas 2nd order tensors (matrices) are denoted as
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}=(Q_{ik})\in\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}^{\otimes 2}
, which will be identified with linear maps
\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{V})
on
\mathcal{V}

via the matrix-vector product by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}=(y_{i})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}=(Q_{ik}x_{k})

(Einstein’s summation convention used).

Tensors of higher order than two will be denoted as
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{w}}{\textstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{w}}=\mathrm{w}_{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}\dots i_{q}}=\mathrm{w}_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathcal{V}^{\otimes q}
for a tensor of order
q
. Considering a tensor
of even order, say
2q
:
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{a}}{\textstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{a}}=(\mathrm{a}_{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}\dots i_{q}j_{1}j_{2}j_{3}\dots j_{q}})=(\mathrm{a}_{\mathfrak{i}\mathfrak{j}})\in\mathcal{V}^{\otimes 2q}
— where for ease of notation we have
used a multi-index
\mathfrak{i}=(i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}\dots i_{q})\in\mathbb{N}^{q}
—
it can be naturally be viewed as a linear map
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{A}}{\textstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{A}}\in\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{V}^{\otimes q})

on the space of tensors of half the order, by contracting the tensor product
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{a}}{\textstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{a}}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{w}}{\textstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{w}}
over the last
q
indices, thereby implicitly using the canonical
Euclidean inner product to be introduced below:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{A}}{\textstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{A}}:\;\mathcal{V}^{\otimes q}\ni\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{w}}{\textstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{w}}\mapsto(\sum_{j_{1}\dots j_{q}}\mathrm{a}_{i_{1}\dots i_{q}j_{1}\dots j_{q}}\mathrm{w}_{j_{1}\dots j_{q}})=\sum_{\mathfrak{j}}(\mathrm{a}_{\mathfrak{i}\mathfrak{j}}\mathrm{w}_{\mathfrak{j}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{A}}{\textstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{A}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{w}}{\textstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{w}}\in\mathcal{V}^{\otimes q},

(1)

where the last sum is particularly suggestive, as it is a matrix-vector multiplication with
multi-indices. By choosing bases in
\mathcal{V}^{\otimes 2q}
, or, equivalently,
by ordering the multi-indices in some way,
one may easily construct a matrix representation for the map
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{A}}{\textstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{A}}
. For the elasticity
tensor this will be done in Section
2.2
by using the so-called
Kelvin notation
.

Inner product and symmetry:

The canonical Euclidean inner product for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}\in\mathcal{V}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
denoted by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}=\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}\right\rangle:=x_{i}y_{i}
.
This induces an inner product for general tensors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{v}}{\textstyle\mathbf{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{v}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{w}}{\textstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{w}}\in\mathcal{V}^{\otimes q}
,
namely
\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{v}}{\textstyle\mathbf{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{v}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{w}}{\textstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle:=(\mathrm{v}_{j_{1}\dots j_{q}}\mathrm{w}_{j_{1}\dots j_{q}})
.
If the map
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{A}}{\textstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{A}}\in\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{V}^{\otimes q})
in Eq.(
1
)
induced by a tensor of twice the order is symmetric w.r.t. this inner product, i.e. if

\forall\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{v}}{\textstyle\mathbf{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{v}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{w}}{\textstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{w}}:\quad\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{A}}{\textstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{A}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{v}}{\textstyle\mathbf{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{v}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{w}}{\textstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{v}}{\textstyle\mathbf{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{v}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{A}}{\textstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{A}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{w}}{\textstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle,

(2)

then the tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{a}}{\textstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{a}}
is
physically symmetric

(here
(\mathrm{a}_{\mathfrak{i}\mathfrak{j}})=(\mathrm{a}_{\mathfrak{j}\mathfrak{i}})
). And if in addition

\forall\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{w}}{\textstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{w}}\neq\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{0}}{\textstyle\mathbf{0}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{0}}:\quad\left\langle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{A}}{\textstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{A}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{w}}{\textstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{w}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{w}}{\textstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle>0,

(3)

the map
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{A}}{\textstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{A}}
and the tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{a}}{\textstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{a}}
are
symmetric positive definite
(SPD).

It is not difficult to see that SPD maps resp. matrices form an open convex cone, denoted by

\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{V}^{\otimes q})
, in the subspace
\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathcal{V}^{\otimes q})
of symmetric
linear maps resp. matrices.
This is actually a differentiable manifold (e.g.
[
92
,
116
,
117
]
),
and its tangent space at the identity is the ambient space of symmetric maps,
in this context sometimes denoted by

\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{V}^{\otimes q}):=\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathcal{V}^{\otimes q})\subset\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{V}^{\otimes q})
.
If the underlying vector space is just some copy of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, one often writes

\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(n)
and
\mathfrak{sym}(n)
instead of
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
The class of even-order SPD tensors is the one of interest here, and the elasticity tensor
to be introduced below is in this sense symmetric, and also usually positive definite, i.e. SPD,
see also
[
118
]
.

Behaviour under rotation and invariance:

A rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{O}(\mathcal{V})
(
\mathrm{O}
denotes the orthogonal group,
see Appendix
B
) (of the coordinate
system) in
\mathcal{V}
,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}
induces a corresponding
transformation of a higher-order tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{w}}{\textstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{w}}\in\mathcal{V}^{\otimes q}
,
this is sometimes termed the Rayleigh product
[
130
]
:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{\hat{w}}}{\textstyle\mathbf{\hat{w}}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{\hat{w}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\hat{w}}}=(\hat{\mathrm{w}}_{\ell_{1}\dots\ell_{q}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\star\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{w}}{\textstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{w}}:=(Q_{\ell_{1}i_{1}}\dots Q_{\ell_{q}i_{q}}\mathrm{w}_{i_{1}\dots i_{q}})\in\mathcal{V}^{\otimes q}.

(4)

In case an even-order tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{a}}{\textstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{a}}
does not change under a rotation, i.e. in case

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{\hat{a}}}{\textstyle\mathbf{\hat{a}}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{\hat{a}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\hat{a}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\star\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{a}}{\textstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{a}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{a}}{\textstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{a}},

(5)

one says that it is
invariant
under the rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}
, or in a more fancy way, invariant
under the subgroup
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}}\subset\mathrm{O}(\mathcal{V})
generated by

\{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}},-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\}
. The maximal subgroup
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{a}}{\textstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{a}}}\subseteq\mathrm{O}(\mathcal{V})

under which such a tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{a}}{\textstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{a}}
is invariant
is termed the
symmetry class
of the tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{a}}{\textstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{a}}
. It is obvious that any
even-order tensor is invariant under the inversion
-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}
, i.e. the subgroup

\{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}},-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}\}
, so that always
\{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}},-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}\}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{a}}{\textstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{a}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{a}}}
.

2.2
Hooke’s law in Kelvin’s matrix notation

Turning now to the case of linear elasticity, the 4th order elasticity tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}
acts
on the symmetric infinitesimal strain
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
to yield the Cauchy stress tensor

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}:\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}};

(6)

see also Appendix
C.1
for details on the notation.
The Eq.(
6
) is Hooke’s generalised law.
Both stress and strain tensors lie in the space

\mathcal{S}:=\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathcal{V})=\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{V})\subset\mathcal{V}^{\otimes 2}

of symmetric 2nd order tensors, i.e.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
, and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
; whereas the
fourth order elasticity tensor

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}\in\mathcal{E}:=\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathcal{S})\subset\mathcal{V}^{\otimes 4}
may be
seen as an SPD map on
\mathcal{S}
.

Choosing a particular basis in
\mathcal{S}
, one may write Hooke’s law in matrix-vector
notation, where the coefficient array of that basis is in the
representation space
\mathcal{T}=\mathbb{R}^{k}
, with
k=\dim\mathcal{S}
, thereby establishing a linear one-to-one
correspondence
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}
(see Appendix
C.1
), which in 3D reads as

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}

\displaystyle:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}):=[\sigma_{11},\sigma_{22},\sigma_{33},\sqrt{2}\,\sigma_{23},\sqrt{2}\,\sigma_{13},\sqrt{2}\,\sigma_{12}]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\in\mathcal{T}

(7)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}

\displaystyle:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}):=[\varepsilon_{11},\varepsilon_{22},\varepsilon_{33},\sqrt{2}\,\varepsilon_{23},\sqrt{2}\,\varepsilon_{13},\sqrt{2}\,\varepsilon_{12}]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\in\mathcal{T}.

(8)

This notation goes back to Kelvin
[
177
,
178
]
, see also
[
75
,
76
,
113
,
118
]
. In 2D one has

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}

\displaystyle:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}):=[\sigma_{11},\sigma_{22},\sqrt{2}\,\sigma_{12}]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\in\mathcal{T}

(9)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}

\displaystyle:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}):=[\varepsilon_{11},\varepsilon_{22},\sqrt{2}\,\varepsilon_{12}]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\in\mathcal{T}.

(10)

By a slight abuse of notation, the map
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}
is again denoted the
same as in 3D.

The linear relation in Hooke’s law Eq.(
6
) in
\mathcal{T}
becomes

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}},

(11)

with an SPD Kelvin matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathcal{T})
, thereby establishing a linear map

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}:\mathcal{E}\to\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathcal{T})
,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}})
. In terms of the components
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}=(c_{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}i_{4}})
, in 3D the Kelvin matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
and thus the map
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}

is given by

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}):={\small\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{c}_{1111}&\mathrm{c}_{1122}&\mathrm{c}_{1133}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{c}_{1123}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{c}_{1113}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{c}_{1112}\\
&\mathrm{c}_{2222}&\mathrm{c}_{2233}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{c}_{2223}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{c}_{2213}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{c}_{2212}\\
&&\mathrm{c}_{3333}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{c}_{3323}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{c}_{3313}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{c}_{3312}\\
&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathrm{c}_{2323}&\;\;\;2\,\mathrm{c}_{2313}&\;\;\;2\,\mathrm{c}_{2312}\\
&\mathrm{SYM}&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathrm{c}_{1313}&\;\;\;2\,\mathrm{c}_{1312}\\
&&&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathrm{c}_{1212}\end{bmatrix}},

(12)

while in 2D one obtains

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}):={\small\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{c}_{1111}&\mathrm{c}_{1122}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{c}_{1112}\\
&\mathrm{c}_{2222}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{c}_{2212}\\
\mathrm{SYM}&&\;\;\;2\,\mathrm{c}_{1212}\end{bmatrix}}.

(13)

2.3
Group reduction and local spectral decomposition

The elasticity class
[
133
,
28
,
31
,
96
,
19
]

of the material or the elasticity tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}\in\mathcal{E}

[
100
,
101
]
is determined
by rotating the coordinate system
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{x}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}
,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{O}(\mathcal{V})

in such a way as to reduce the number of constants in the 4th order elasticity
tensor
\hat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\star\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}\in\mathcal{E}
by introducing as many zeros
as possible. The tensor in this
form then turns out to be invariant under a whole subgroup of rotations
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}}\subseteq\mathrm{O}(\mathcal{V})
, which is called the symmetry or invariance group of the
material resp. elasticity tensor. For simplicity and without loss of generality,
and as more is not needed in our modelling
approach, we restrict ourselves here to
proper
rotations, i.e. the subgroup

\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})\subset\mathrm{O}(\mathcal{V})
of special orthogonal matrices, i.e. orthogonal
matrices
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}
with
\det\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}=1
. The symmetry or invariance group of a tensor will
therefore in the following also be only considered as a subgroup of
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
.

Transformations of the Kelvin matrix:

As just mentioned, a rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
of the coordinate system in
\mathcal{V}
induces a
transformation of the stress and strain tensors

[
113
,
114
]
and on the 4th order elasticity tensor

\hat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\star\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}
, as described for the general case in Eq.(
4
).

In this context it is important to note that the linear map
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}

defined in the preceding Section
2.2
in Eqs.(
7
) and (
8
)
in 3D, and in Eqs.(
9
) and (
10
) in 2D, is actually unitary, i.e. it preserves inner products; in fact this is here the advantage of the Kelvin notation over the
better known Voigt notation. This also means that for a rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
in
physical space, which one may recall preserves the inner product in
\mathcal{V}
,
there is a one-to-one correspondence
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}:\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})\to\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})

[
113
,
114
]
to a rotation

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}})\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
, which in turn preserves the inner product in
\mathcal{T}
.
Concretely this means that when
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\star\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
and

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\star\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
, and

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}),\,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}),\,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}}),\,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{vrep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}),\;\text{ then }\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}},

(14)

as well as

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}}:\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}}:\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\varepsilon}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}};\;\text{ and }\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{s}}},\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{e}}}.

(15)

It is not too difficult to see that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}
is actually an injective group homomorphism.
Thus the map
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}:\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})\to\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
is a unitary representation of

\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
on
\mathcal{T}
, see e.g.
[
46
,
86
,
90
]
—
and the precise definition of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}
may be found in Appendix
B.3
. Hence the
image of
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
under
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}
, which will be denoted by

\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T}):=\im\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V}))\subseteq\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
, is a Lie subgroup
of
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
.

As is well known, e.g. see Appendix
B.3
, the Lie algebra of a Lie group
is the tangent vector space at the identity, and it has usually the same notation
as the Lie group, but with small Fraktur letters; i.e.
\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{V})
is the
Lie algebra of
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
. The Lie algebra of
\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
is denoted by
\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})
.
The dimension of
\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V}))
, which is the dimension of its Lie
algebra
\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})
, is due to the injectivity of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}
that of
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})

resp.
\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{V})
, i.e.
\dim(\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{V}))=\dim(\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T}))
.
The injective group homomorphism
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}:\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})\to\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})

induces an injective linear map on the corresponding
Lie algebras
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}:\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{V})\to\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})
, such that
\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}(\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{V}))
,
details of which may also be found in Appendix
B.3
.

This implies that for a coordinate transform
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
, which transforms the elasticity
tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}\in\mathcal{E}
according to Eq.(
4
) as

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{\hat{c}}}{\textstyle\mathbf{\hat{c}}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{\hat{c}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\hat{c}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\star\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}
, and whose induced transformation on
\mathcal{T}
is

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}})\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
, the effect on the Kelvin matrix

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}})\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathcal{T})
is given by

[
113
,
30
,
31
,
32
]

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{C}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{C}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{C}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\hat{C}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\star\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{\hat{c}}}{\textstyle\mathbf{\hat{c}}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{\hat{c}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\hat{c}}}).

(16)

Group reduction:

After this preparation one may address the group reduction,
see e.g.
[
46
,
86
]
and
[
133
,
28
,
31
,
96
,
19
,
100
,
101
]
.
As already indicated, one seeks a rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})

such that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{\mathring{c}}}{\textstyle\mathbf{\mathring{c}}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{\mathring{c}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\mathring{c}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\star\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}
has as many zeros as possible.
For the corresponding Kelvin matrix
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{\mathring{c}}}{\textstyle\mathbf{\mathring{c}}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{\mathring{c}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\mathring{c}}})\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathcal{T})
this means (cf. Eq.(
16
)) that

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}=\diag(\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{j})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}

(17)

has block diagonal form with diagonal blocks
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{j}
, see e.g.
[
46
]
.
This
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
is called the group reduction of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}
,
and the blocks
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{j}
on the diagonal indicate invariant subspaces of

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})
.
The non-diagonal minimum non-zero pattern of

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
is characteristic for the elasticity class,
and invariant under the rotations
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{V}})\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
with

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{V}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}}\subseteq\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
. In fact, the elasticity classes are commonly
discussed in this group reduced form
[
133
,
28
,
31
,
96
,
19
,
100
,
101
]
.
When considering random choices resp. a stochastic model for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
, it may be
observed that the mean or expected value may be invariant under a larger
group
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}},m}
for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}\in\mathcal{S}
resp.
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}},m}
for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\in\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})

[
101
]
, i.e.
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}},m}
and

\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}},m}
. Some possible choices for a mean will be
investigated in Sections
3.2
and
3.3
.

Observe that a completely diagonal form with a diagonal
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
would mean that
Eq.(
17
) is an eigenvalue or spectral decomposition.
But it is usually not possible to achieve a completely diagonal form just with rotations

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
, see also
[
91
,
82
]
,
the general reason for which will be touched on now.

As already remarked in Appendix
B.3
, the dimension of
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
for
d>1
smaller than that of
\mathcal{T}
—
\dim\mathcal{V}<\dim\mathcal{T}=\dim\mathcal{S}=\dim(\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathcal{V}))
— which implies that the dimension of the Lie group
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
,
is also smaller than that of the Lie group
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
, i.e.
\dim(\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{V}))<\dim(\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T}))
. This in turn means that
\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
is a proper
subgroup of the full Lie group
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
of proper rotations on
\mathcal{T}
,
i.e.
\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
. A similar relation obviously holds for its Lie algebra,

\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})
and
\dim(\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T}))=\dim(\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{V}))<\dim(\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T}))
.

Spectral decomposition:

The group reduction just discussed commonly produces a block-diagonal matrix
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
.
In order for
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathcal{T})
in Eq.(
17
) to completely diagonalise

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathcal{T})
in general,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}
would need access to the whole group
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
,
but one is only allowed
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
resulting from purely spatial rotations

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}})
with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
, which as just explained is only
a subgroup. Still, Eq.(
17
) is a good start, and
one can now proceed a step further by noting that
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathcal{T})

is symmetric and thus has a spectral decomposition

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}

(18)

with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}
a diagonal matrix of eigenvalues of

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
. As
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
is block-diagonal, a similar structure will
appear in
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
, in fact Eq.(
18
) are separate
eigenproblems for each block
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{j}
from the group decomposition Eq.(
17
),
see e.g.
[
46
]
.

As
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})

is SPD, so is its rotated form
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
, and hence

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\in\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
is diagonally positive; such matrices
are denoted by
\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\in\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
is obviously also the matrix
of eigenvalues of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
, and equating Eq.(
17
) with
Eq.(
18
), one obtains

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\;\Rightarrow\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}},

(19)

where
