\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
. But
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})

is a group, and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{-1}\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
, so that

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
, and hence Eq.(
19
)
is a spectral or eigenvalue decomposition of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
; this equation will later
form the basis for its representation.

Group invariance:

Coming back to Eq.(
17
), and recollecting Eq.(
5
),
invariance under some symmetry group
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}}\subseteq\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
for the
reduced elasticity tensor
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}}
means that

\forall\,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}}:\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}\star\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}}=\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}}.

(20)

For the Kelvin matrix in the group reduced block diagonal form
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}

with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}}
and its associated
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}})\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}})
,
from Eq.(
16
) this translates to

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
, and hence
[
46
]

\forall\,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}:\;\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}},

(21)

where the mapped symmetry or invariance subgroup was designated as

\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}})\subset\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
.
What Eq.(
21
) says is that
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
and all
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}

commute, i.e. they are co-axial and can all be simultaneously
diagonalised with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
, see also Eq.(
18
).

It may be noted that the Eq.(
21
) is homogeneous linear in
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
.
As
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})
, the equations Eq.(
21
)
determine a subspace
\mathfrak{sym}[\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}]\subseteq\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})
in the vector space
of all symmetric matrices. As already mentioned,
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
is an open convex cone in
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})
; and thus
the Kelvin matrices which are invariant under
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
, and hence belong to the same
elasticity class, are on the intersection of said subspace
\mathfrak{sym}[\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}]
of
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})

and the cone
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
, i.e. they all lie in the cone

\mathfrak{sym}[\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}]\cap\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})
.

2.4
Random tensor fields

Let
\mathcal{G}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded domain in a
d
-dimensional Euclidean space

\mathbb{R}^{d}\equiv\mathcal{V}
(here
d=2
or
d=3
) where we want to model or generate a field of
elasticity tensors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{G}
. Rather than the elasticity tensor,
here we want equivalently to model the corresponding Kelvin matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Vrep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}})
.
As the exact value of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
at a location

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{G}
may be uncertain, either due to a highly heterogeneous material, or simply a
lack of knowledge about the exact value, we assume that a probabilistic model is adopted,
so that the Kelvin matrix field is modelled as a in a probability sense second order tensor
valued random field (having first and second moments), i.e. a measurable mapping

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega):\mathcal{G}\times\Omega\to\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})

(22)

on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathfrak{F},\mathbb{P})
, where
\Omega
represents the sample space
containing all outcomes
\omega\in\Omega
,
\mathfrak{F}
is the
\sigma
-algebra of
measurable subsets of
\Omega
,
\mathbb{P}
a probability measure, and the corresponding
expectation operator is denoted by
\mathbb{E}\left(\cdot\right)
. To avoid unnecessary
clutter in the notation, the argument
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{G}
will be omitted, unless it is necessary
for understanding. The same will often be true of the argument
\omega\in\Omega
.

Mean field and covariance function:

The first and second order probabilistic descriptors of such a field would be the mean field,
which allows to split the field into the deterministic mean
\,\overline{\!{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})

and the zero mean random part
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{C}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{C}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{C}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{C}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
, and the covariance function

\displaystyle\,\overline{\!{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot)\right)=\int_{\Omega}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)\;\mathbb{P}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega),

(23)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{C}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{C}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{C}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{C}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)

\displaystyle:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)-\,\overline{\!{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}),

(24)

\displaystyle\text{cov}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{C}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{C}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{C}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{C}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot)\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{C}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{C}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{C}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{C}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}},\cdot)\right).

(25)

The material symmetry-group properties are now encoded in the mean and covariance
function Eqs.(
23
) and (
25
),
see e.g.
[
101
]
. Here one may see another
advantage of numerically working with the Kelvin matrix — a 2nd order tensor — which
results in a covariance which is a 4th order valued tensor function
on
\mathcal{T}
,
although with some symmetries. In case that one would form
the analogue of Eq.(
25
) for the elasticity tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
, i.e.

\text{cov}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{\tilde{c}}}{\textstyle\mathbf{\tilde{c}}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{\tilde{c}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\tilde{c}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot)\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{\tilde{c}}}{\textstyle\mathbf{\tilde{c}}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{\tilde{c}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\tilde{c}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}},\cdot)\right),

(26)

this would be an 8th order tensor on
\mathcal{V}
,
although with many symmetries,
i.e. a much larger object.

In both cases this large number of covariance component functions
encode simultaneously the spatial correlations
and
the symmetry-group invariances.
Here we shall follow a different route, and, as will become clear in the
following Section
3
,
encode the symmetry-group properties of the random Kelvin matrix in a field of
n

real parameters
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a map
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
.
As will be seen later,
the number of parameters is at maximum
n=\dim(\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T}))+\dim(\mathfrak{diag}(\mathcal{T}))
.

The first and second order probabilistic descriptors of these parameters then are

\displaystyle\,\overline{\!{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot)\right)=\int_{\Omega}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)\;\mathbb{P}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega),

(27)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)

\displaystyle:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)-\,\overline{\!{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}),

(28)

\displaystyle\text{cov}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot)\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}},\cdot)\right),

(29)

and they then only carry the information about the spatial and probabilistic distribution.
The maximum number of covariance component functions in Eq.(
29
)
is thus
n^{2}
, but again with the normal symmetry of a symmetric matrix, and thus
only
n(n+1)/2
. This is actually the number of independent components in both
Eqs.(
25
) and (
26
).
Additionally, and more importantly,
as will be shown in the following Section
3
, these parameters allow
one to separate the information about spatial orientation, stiffness or moduli, and eigenstrain
distribution through the Lie product representation, cf. Section
3.1
.

Spatial modelling of parameter fields:

The parameters will be described in general terms in Section
3.4
, and then in
detail for each elasticity class in Section
4
. To arrive at a spatial model
for the random parameter field
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
, one usually aims at a
separated

or tensor product representation in a sum or integral of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
, e.g.
[
108
]
.

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)=\sum_{\ell}\alpha_{\ell}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}_{\ell}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\zeta_{\ell}(\omega),\;\text{ or }\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)=\int_{\Xi}\alpha(\xi)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\xi)\zeta(\omega,\xi)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

(30)

Such separated representations actually follow from fairly general methods to analyse
and represent parametric objects like the fields in Eq.(
30
), where the random
event
\omega
may be regarded as the parameter, resulting
in tensor-product like expressions
[
110
,
111
]
.

Here
\zeta_{\ell}(\omega)
resp.
\zeta(\omega,\xi)
are usually zero mean unit variance random variables,

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}_{\ell}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
resp.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\xi)
are possibly normalised purely spatial functions, and

\alpha_{\ell}
resp.
\alpha(\xi)
are some scaling coefficients.

Aiming for example at a Karhunen-Loève expansion of the spatial field
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)

(see e.g.
[
108
]
), one has to solve the Fredholm integral eigenproblem with
the covariance function
\text{cov}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})
from Eq.(
29
) as integral kernel

\int_{\mathcal{G}}\text{cov}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})\,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}_{\ell}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}=\mu_{\ell}\,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}_{\ell}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}),

(31)

for positive eigenvalues
\mu_{\ell}
and eigenvector fields
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}_{\ell}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Here it was tacitly assumed that the integral operator in
Eq.(
31
) is self-adjoint and positive and has a discrete spectrum, which would be usually
the case with a bounded domain
\mathcal{G}
and continuous
\text{cov}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})
for example.

It should be noted that spatial
stochastic
homogeneity would be evident as usual
from the fact that
\text{cov}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})
would be a function of only the difference of
the arguments,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}}:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}
,
say
\text{cov}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{K}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{K}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{K}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{K}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}})
. In that case the eigenproblem Eq.(
31
)
is a convolution equation, and it is well known how to solve those by Fourier
methods, as indeed the Fourier functions are (possibly generalised) eigenfunction
of the integral operator with kernel
\text{cov}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}}
represented by Eq.(
31
).

Indeed, with the eigenfunctions known, the eigenvalues are obtained by a Fourier
transform — continuous or in form of a series, depending on the integral operator
Eq.(
31
) and the domain
\mathcal{G}
— of the matrix function
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{K}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{K}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{K}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}})
above.
This Fourier transform
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{K}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{K}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{K}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\hat{K}}}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}})
, where
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}
is the vector of wave numbers resp. spatial frequencies dual to
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}}
, is the SPD
spectral matrix
. Locally diagonalised for
each
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}
, it then displays the eigenvalues of the Karhunen-Loève eigenproblem Eq.(
31
).
This is actually the well know spectral description of stochastically homogeneous random fields.
Further information may be found in
[
108
]
and the references
therein; see also the remarks in Section
3.4
. In the general —
non-stochastically homogeneous — case, the Karhunen-Loève eigenproblem Eq.(
31
) has to
be solved numerically, cf. e.g.
[
106
,
105
]
.

In any case, the orthonormal eigenfunctions
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}_{\ell}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
determine uncorrelated
mean zero unit variance
random variables
\zeta_{\ell}(\omega)
through the projection of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
,
setting
\alpha_{\ell}=\sqrt{\mu_{\ell}}
,
onto the spatial eigenvector basis
\{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}_{\ell}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
:

\zeta_{\ell}(\omega)=\frac{1}{\alpha_{\ell}}\;\int_{\mathcal{G}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}_{\ell}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}.

(32)

With this one has all the ingredients for the spatial representation in Eq.(
30
),
which is in this case called a Karhunen-Loève expansion, equivalent to a singular value decomposition
of the random field
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
viewed as an operator kernel, e.g.
[
108
]
.

As may be noted immediately, for Gaussian random fields all the uncorrelated random
variables
\zeta_{\ell}
in Eq.(
32
) are also Gaussian, and thus they are also
independent. For modelling purposes,
this is what is usually desired, a model in independent random variables.
As the parameter vector field
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}
effectively models the log of the SPD tensor field,
this partly explains the allure and pre-dominance of log-normal models for
such SPD tensors
[
158
]
. For cases where the log-parameters
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}

are not normal, the random variables
\zeta_{\ell}
are merely uncorrelated, but not independent.
In this case it is possible to express these random variables in Norbert Wiener’s

polynomial chaos
expansion, cf. e.g.
[
106
,
108
,
105
]

and the references therein.
Thus one may represent
\zeta_{\ell}(\omega)
by a series of multi-variate polynomials

\Psi_{\mathfrak{a}}
—
\mathfrak{a}
is typically a multi-index —
in independent mean zero unit variance Gaussian random variables

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\omega)=(\xi_{1}(\omega),\dots,\xi_{j}(\omega),\dots)
as

\zeta_{\ell}(\omega)=\sum_{\mathfrak{a}}\beta^{\ell}_{\mathfrak{a}}\,\Psi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\omega)),

(33)

with some coefficients
\beta^{\ell}_{\mathfrak{a}}
. This then yields the field
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}
in
Eq.(
30
) as a function of
independent
standard
Gaussian variables
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\omega)
:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)=\sum_{\ell}\alpha_{\ell}\left(\sum_{\mathfrak{a}}\beta^{\ell}_{\mathfrak{a}}\,\Psi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\omega))\right)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}_{\ell}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})=\sum_{\mathfrak{a}}\Psi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\omega))\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{\mathfrak{a}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}),

(34)

where the expression
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{\mathfrak{a}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}):=\sum_{\ell}\alpha_{\ell}\beta^{\ell}_{a}\,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}_{\ell}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})

may be seen as the polynomial chaos expansion coefficient.

The foregoing covers the modelling aspect.

To perform the reverse task,
the inverse problem of conditioning

[
123
,
172
,
170
]
, one has to
change

the
descriptions in Eqs.(
30
),(
33
) and (
34
)
, e.g. spatial functions
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}_{\ell}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
and the random variables
\zeta_{\ell}(\omega)

such that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
has the proper statistics at each
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{G}
.

This can range from finding the SPD tensor closest to measurements

[
49
,
120
,
185
]
to conditioning the
random SPD tensor field
[
151
,
150
,
107
,
112
,
109
,
132
,
156
]
.
One may note that some of the filtering methods — e.g. the Gauss-Markov-Kalman filter —
for Bayesian conditioning work directly
on the polynomial chaos expansion coefficients
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{\mathfrak{a}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
in Eq.(
34
)

[
144
,
152
,
150
,
107
,
112
,
109
,
80
]
.

3
Modelling of Elasticity Tensors

The main idea from the preceding Section
2.3
for the modelling
approach to be discussed here is contained in the
Eqs.(
17
),(
18
) and (
19
), which are collected here for easy
reference:

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}},\quad\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\;\Rightarrow\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}},\;\text{ with }\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}.

(35)

Here
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
is the group reduced form of the Kelvin matrix

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
, and

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}})\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
and hence

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
as well, and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\in\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
.

3.1
Lie representation

Thus, given
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
in the form of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
, which means
in form of an eigenstrain distribution
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}
and eigenvalues
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}
, plus an orientation

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
in space which describes the rotation from
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
to
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
,
one may form
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}})
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}
and then generate
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
:

(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}})\mapsto(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}})\mapsto(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}})\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}.

(36)

This is a mapping from
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})\times\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})\times\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
to
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
,
resp. from
\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\times\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})\times\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
to
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})

Lie group representation of the Kelvin matrix:

The representation Eq.(
36
) will be called the Lie group representation of the Kelvin
matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
, as
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
resp.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})

and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
are elements
from matrix Lie groups. But the diagonal SPD matrices
\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
form a commutative
or Abelian Lie group under matrix multiplication, and hence
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\in\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
is also from a
Lie group. The Lie representation in Eq.(
36
) is thus one on a
product
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}
of Lie groups
[
89
,
158
,
62
,
61
,
160
]
,
which is again a Lie group:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}:=\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\times\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})\times\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T}).

(37)

It should be noted here that the representation
in Eq.(
36
) is
not unique
. What is unique is the spectrum of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
,
i.e. the set of diagonal elements of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\in\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
. They could be re-ordered,
inducing a re-ordering of the columns of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
. This could be avoided by
insisting on an ordering, say by size, of the diagonal elements
\lambda_{i}
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}
.
But even more significantly, when degenerate or multiple eigenvalues occur, as is always
the case for the ‘higher’ elasticity classes (cf. Section
4
), those eigenvalues
are associated with invariant subspaces of dimension two or higher. This makes the quest for
an orthonormal eigenvector basis of such an invariant subspace — the associated columns
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
— a matter of arbitrary choice.

As further detailed in Appendix
A.2
, for a general Lie group
G
, its
Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, the tangent space at the identity, plays an important role

[
41
,
167
,
87
,
1
]
.
Specifically, it is possible to use the Lie exponential map — for matrix Lie groups and algebras
this is the usual matrix exponential —
\exp:\mathfrak{g}\to G
to represent elements of the
group
G
on a the free vector space
\mathfrak{g}
; the inverse of this map is the
\log:G\to\mathfrak{g}
.
Here we want to parametrise the product Lie group Eq.(
37
) in this way
via its Lie algebra.

Exponential Lie algebra representation of the Kelvin matrix:

The exponential and the logarithm allow one to shift the representation from the Lie group
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}

in Eq.(
37
) to its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{s}_{p}
, which is a combination of the Lie algebras
of the individual factors:

\mathfrak{s}_{p}=\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})\times\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})\times\mathfrak{diag}(\mathcal{T}).

(38)

Here
\mathfrak{diag}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})
is the vector space of diagonal matrices,
the Lie algebra of
\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
.
With the exponential map, this finally yields
a representation of the Kelvin matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
on the vector space
\mathfrak{s}_{p}
in Eq.(
38
).
It allows one also to separate the effects of spatial orientation,
represented by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
, strain distribution, as
represented by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
, and stiffness, as given by

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
. In Section
3.2
and Appendix
A.2

this is used further to define the ‘elasticity metric’,
which is used in the sequel for Fréchet means.

It should be noted that to represent
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}\in\mathcal{E}
resp.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
,
usually not all of
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
resp.
\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
is required, as one has invariance
w.r.t. rotations
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}}
resp.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{W}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}})\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}

in the symmetry group of the material.
We shall not introduce the group theoretic complication which might arise from a transition
to factor groups here

[
99
,
100
,
101
]
,
and allow possibly

wrapped
representations, as they occur also often in circular statistics, cf. e.g.
[
84
,
83
]
. A similar ‘wrapping problem’
occurs in the representation of spatial rotations
\mathrm{SO}(n)
on its Lie algebra

\mathfrak{so}(n)
via the exponential map,
see also the remarks at the end of Appendix
B.2
.

As is easily seen from Eq.(
19
) and Eq.(
17
) and the above discussion,
the group invariance properties of the symmetric tensor

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}=\log\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\in\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})

(39)

and its exponential
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}
are the same, a fact which was also used in

[
70
,
71
]
. And as we are using the
Lie algebra
\mathfrak{diag}(\mathcal{T})
of the Lie group
\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
, we are essentially
modelling
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}=\log\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
in Eq.(
39
).

Note that one should actually
apply some scaling prior to using the logarithm in Eq.(
39
), as the
quantities involved may have physical dimensions. One could use in Eq.(
39
)
a diagonal scaling matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}\in\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
— often this is just a multiple
of the identity,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}=\alpha\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}
for some
\alpha>0
— of the same physical dimensions as

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}
and then compute with

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}=\log\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}^{-1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}^{-1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\in\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T}).

(40)

Or one could use a reference matrix

\widehat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
of the same physical dimensions as
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
— often

\widehat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}\in\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
is a diagonal matrix, or even just a multiple
of the identity
\widehat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}=\alpha\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}
as above — and define the logarithm as

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}=\log(\widehat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}^{-1/2}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\widehat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}^{-1/2})\in\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T}),

(41)

see also e.g.
[
160
]
. It is tacitly assumed that some such transformation to
non-dimensional quantities has been carried out, so there is no need to clutter the notation with it.

Together with the exponential Rodrigues formulas in Appendix
B
,
this means that all the invariance constraints
in the Lie group representation Eq.(
36
)
may be applied on the Lie algebra level in Eq.(
38
), but
a little refinement is in order here. As in the product Lie group
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}
the
first factor
\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
is contained in the second one, it is clear that in
the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{s}_{p}=\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})\times\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})\times\mathfrak{diag}(\mathcal{T})

one has
\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})
, and so the second factor can be reduced to

\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})^{\perp}\subset\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})
, the orthogonal complement of
\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})
in
\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})
.
Hence we may use for the representation the direct sum of Lie algebras

\mathfrak{r}=\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})\oplus\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})^{\perp}\oplus\mathfrak{diag}(\mathcal{T}).

(42)

Hence in order that one may represent the Kelvin matrix later in detail
in Section
4
, the following general picture emerges:
starting with a triple
(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}})\in\mathfrak{r}

one first builds the logarithm
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}
in factored form, and then
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
:

\displaystyle(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}})\quad

\displaystyle\longmapsto\quad(\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}},\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}})\quad\longmapsto\quad\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\log\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}

(43)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\quad\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}},\phantom{Hhhhhhhhhhhhhh}

(44)

where, as before,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}
,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}
,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}
, and

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}
; in between one has
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}=\log\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\in\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})
, and finally

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
. This exponential representation

\exp:\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})\to\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
ensures positive definiteness of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
.

As what we finally seek
is a measurable map
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}:\mathcal{G}\times\Omega\to\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
to give the
elasticity Kelvin matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
for a location
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{G}
and event

\omega\in\Omega
, from what was just outlined in Eq.(
43
), this will be done
in several steps. One of them is to produce random tensor fields with values in

\mathfrak{r}
in Eq.(
42
), i.e. random triples

\mathcal{G}\times\Omega\to(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}})\in\mathfrak{r}
.
From this, at each location
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}
and event
\omega
, the log tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}=\log\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
is built according to Eq.(
43
), and then
finally
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}
as just described in Eq.(
44
).

In order to put the choice of random
elements into proper perspective, in the following Sections
3.2
and
3.3

some consideration on how to to choose a suitable kind of mean or expectation operator
on
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}
will be given, together with some requirements for such a choice. Then
we can continue on how to generate random Kelvin matrices in general in Section
3.4
.

3.2
Fréchet means on manifolds

As the set of SPD maps
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
is not a flat vector space, but a manifold
which can be accessed in different ways, this leads
to the question of what kind of average or mean to use for
elements of
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})
, and this is
tied to the question of how distances are measured on this set.
As the ambient space

\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})
is a vector space, it appears at first natural to
calculate averages and means as one would in a vector space, using the common arithmetic
or Euclidean mean. But, as mentioned already in Section
1
, it has been found that by
using this kind of mean one introduces undesirable effects

[
146
,
2
,
116
,
4
,
5
,
43
,
44
,
184
,
54
,
48
]
. As further described there in Section
1
and also in
Appendix
A
, these are the so-called the

swelling, fattening, and shrinking
effects

[
157
,
89
,
158
,
62
,
61
,
48
]
.

Variational characterisation of the mean:

In this context where we want to describe shortly how to generalise the arithmetic or Euclidean mean.
One may note first that the mean has a variational
characterisation, cf. also Appendix
A.1
and
[
139
,
146
,
145
,
117
,
118
,
121
,
48
,
173
,
166
,
50
,
160
,
65
]

for further discussion, which is the key for further generalisation.
This characterisation is reproduced in Appendix
A
for better reference,
see Eqs.(
86
)–(
88
), and it is based
on a minimisation:

\,\overline{\!{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}}}^{(\vartheta)}:=\arg\min_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}\in\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})}\Psi_{\vartheta}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}),

(45)

where the
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}
-based variance
\Psi_{\vartheta}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}})
(cf. also Eq.(
88
))
is the so-called ‘loss function’, here based on the general distance function
resp. metric
\vartheta
. In the simple case of a finite collection of items

\{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}_{j}\}
to be averaged this would be

\Psi_{\vartheta}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}})=\sum_{j}w_{j}\vartheta(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}_{j})^{2}
,
with weights
w_{j}\geq 0
,
\sum_{j}w_{j}=1
.
The usual arithmetic resp. Euclidean mean is obtained
by use of the Euclidean distance
\vartheta_{2}
in
\Psi_{\vartheta}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{X}})
,
see Appendix
A
.
But a Euclidean distance on
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
is not necessarily natural, and it is what seems to
be responsible for the undesirable effects just mentioned. So one has to look for other
distance functions
\vartheta
, cf. also Appendix
A.2
, which to use in
Eq.(
45
) in place of the Euclidean metric

\vartheta_{2}
in the variational characterisation.

Possible alternative means:

There are several possible choices, cf. Appendix
A.2
and
[
139
,
146
,
145
,
117
,
118
,
121
,
48
,
173
,
166
,
50
,
160
,
65
]
,
as well as the references therein. The connection with Section
3.1
is as follows.
It has been mentioned here before that the set of positive definite matrices
