\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})
is geometrically an open convex cone in
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})
. Hence
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})

can be equipped with the structure of a differentiable manifold.

The representation of
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
on the product Lie group
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}
in Eq.(
37
), and
via the maps Eq.(
43
) finally on the vector space
\mathfrak{s}_{p}
in Eq.(
38
),
allows one to map a Euclidean structure on the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{s}_{p}
onto a Riemannian structure on
the product Lie group
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}
, each factor separately, see Appendix
A
for more details.
The Riemannian structure enables the definition of geodesics — locally shortest paths — and
thus allows to define a distance between two elements of each of the factor groups
as the length of the shortest geodesic between those two elements.

From this one may define a product metric on the product Lie group
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}

[
184
,
89
,
158
,
62
,
61
,
48
,
160
]
,
and take this as the basis for a metric on
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
.
The product Lie group
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}
used here has a finer structure than that used for

\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{V})
in
[
160
]
, or general SPD matrices in

[
89
,
158
,
62
,
61
,
48
]
, which only uses the spectral
decomposition Eq.(
19
), which would correspond to a
representation on only
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})\times\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
.
Here we want to use this finer structure given by the representation in Eq.(
36
),
which includes the invariance resp. symmetry group reduction, in the definition of a new metric,
which is explained in detail in Appendix
A.2
.
It is proposed to call this metric the ‘elasticity metric’ or ‘elasticity distance’
\vartheta_{E}

in Eq.(
95
). It allows
a fine control to measure differences in spatial orientation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
in Eq.(
36
),
differences in strain distributors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
, and differences in Kelvin moduli

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\in\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
, and combine them with different weights (cf. Appendix
A.2
).

It is based on the product distance on
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}
(see Eq.(
93
) in
Appendix
A.2
).
For two elements
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{2}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
with representations

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{j}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{j}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{j}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{j}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{j}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{j}
,
j=1,2
based on

(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}_{j},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{j},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{j})\in\mathfrak{r}
as in Eq.(
43
),
the squared product distance
\tilde{\vartheta}_{E}^{2}
is given by

\tilde{\vartheta}_{E}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{2})^{2}=\vartheta_{L}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{2})^{2}+c_{\mathcal{V}}\vartheta_{R}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2})^{2}+c_{\mathcal{T}}\vartheta_{R}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{2})^{2},

(46)

with two positive tuning constants
c_{\mathcal{V}},c_{\mathcal{T}}
. The distances on the
component Lie groups are:
for the rotation groups the standard Riemannian metric (cf. Eq.(
89
))

\vartheta_{R}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2})=\left\|\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}})\right\|_{F}
with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{j}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
,
and completely analogous also for
\vartheta_{R}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{2})
with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{j}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
;
and for
\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
also the logarithmic Riemannian metric (cf. Eq.(
90
))

\vartheta_{L}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{2}):=\left\|\log\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{1}-\log\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{2}\right\|_{F}=\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{1}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{2}\right\|_{F}
.
Additionally, one has now to worry about the non-uniqueness of the product representation,
to obtain from
\tilde{\vartheta}_{E}
in Eq.(
46
)
the final elasticity distance
\vartheta_{E}
in Eq.(
95
).
Here, as shown in Appendix
A.2
, one minimises over all possible
Lie product group representations
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}
,
and, as the geodesic is only locally a shortest path, over all ‘intermediate stops’;
but for small variations Eq.(
46
) is all what is needed.

To recap quickly Appendix
A
here, with
this elasticity metric one may go back to Eq.(
45
), employing the
variational characterisation with metric
\vartheta
, and replace the Euclidean metric

\vartheta=\vartheta_{2}
with the elasticity metric
\vartheta=\vartheta_{E}

mentioned in Eq.(
95
).
At the end of Appendix
A
, an elasticity Fréchet mean or
expected value
\mathbb{E}_{\vartheta_{E}}
is thus defined in Eq.(
96
)
for a random elasticity tensor, utilising as loss function
the
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}
-based variance
\Psi_{\vartheta_{E}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}})
in Eq.(
97
), which is
based on the elasticity metric
\vartheta_{E}
in Eq.(
94
).

3.3
Requirements for representation and mean

While the introduction of a new metric and mean where motivated in the preceding
Section
3.2
by geometrical considerations, it might be actually worthwhile
to consider what one may require from a metric for elasticity tensors resp. Kelvin matrices,
and even for any kind of numerical representation in general.

As the goal is to be able to define, model, and represent random tensor fields
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)

on some spatial domain
\mathcal{G}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d=2
or
d=3
with typical point

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{G}
, and
\omega\in\Omega
an event in some appropriate probability space

(\Omega,\mathfrak{F},\mathbb{P})
with
\sigma
-algebra
\mathfrak{F}
and probability measure
\mathbb{P}
,
i.e. a measurable map
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}:\mathcal{G}\times\Omega\to\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
, here we formulate
some requirements which in our view have to be satisfied for the modelling, so that
the random tensor field
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
is usable in e.g. stochastic FEM calculations
and uncertainty quantification (e.g.
[
108
,
164
]
and the references therein). See also

[
160
]
for similar remarks regarding second-order SPD random tensor fields.

Representation requirements:

The requirements we suggest should be met is that
at almost all points
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{G}
and realisations
\omega\in\Omega
,

1.

even under numerical approximations like truncation of series resp. numerical quadrature,
the tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
has to be SPD, i.e.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\in\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})
, and
for all
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}\neq\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{0}}\in\mathcal{T}:\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{x}}>0
;

2.

the tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
has to be invariant
under some group of transformations
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}\subseteq\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
,
the invariance or symmetry class of each realisation;

3.

the mean
\,\overline{\!{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}}^{(\vartheta_{D})}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}):=\mathbb{E}_{\vartheta_{D}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot))\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
based on some desirable metric

\vartheta_{D}
has to be invariant under a possibly larger group of transformations

\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}},m}
, with
\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\supseteq\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}},m}\supset\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
,
the invariance or symmetry class of the mean.

The first item is a necessity in order to be able to successfully calculate with the tensor field

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
. The second item makes sure that each realisation has the proper spatial
invariance resp. symmetry or elasticity class. The third item allows to control the elasticity
class of the mean resp. expected value. For the most part the concern here is the analysis,
modelling, and representation of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
at one point
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{G}
and
sample
\omega\in\Omega
, so that for the sake of brevity these arguments will be dropped
in the sequel, just as they have been in most of the description above,
up to the point in Section
3.4
when spatial random fields will finally be considered.

Desiderata for a Fréchet mean:

Turning now to the mean and metric addressed in the third item above,
first note that a change of physical units or to non-dimensional quantities
is a diagonal scaling like Eq.(
40
), and the mean
should scale the same way, i.e. first scaling and the averaging should give the
same result as first averaging and then scaling. Secondly, a change of coordinate system by some

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}})\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
for any
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
should affect
the same change in the mean, i.e. commutativity of averaging and coordinate transform.
And lastly, one may note that Hooke’s law
— in vector notation in Eq.(
129
) — can just as well be formulated
in terms of compliances, i.e.

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}^{-1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}};\quad\text{ with compliance }\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}^{-1}.

(47)

Hence it seems desirable to require the ‘mean material law’ to be independent of the arbitrariness
of formulating the material law, e.g. Eq.(
129
) or Eq.(
47
),
and therefore demand an appropriate invariance under inversion, i.e. averaging and then inverting should give the same result as first inverting and then averaging.
In simplest terms, given
n
Kelvin matrices
\{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
,
let
\,\overline{\!{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{j}}}^{(\vartheta_{D})}
denote their Fréchet mean based on some
desirable distance
\vartheta_{D}
, cf. Appendix
A
, and let
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}})\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
,

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
be an arbitrary spatially induced rotation.
The requirements for the mean then are:

1.

invariance under scaling:
\forall\;\alpha>0
:

\,\overline{\!{(\alpha\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{j})}}^{(\vartheta_{D})}=\alpha\,\,\overline{\!{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{j}}}^{(\vartheta_{D})}
;

2.

invariance under orthogonal transformations:

\,\overline{\!{(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{j}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}})}}^{(\vartheta_{D})}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\,\overline{\!{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{j}}}^{(\vartheta_{D})}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
;

3.

invariance under inversion: let
\{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}_{j}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{j}^{-1}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})

with mean
\,\overline{\!{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}_{j}}}^{(\vartheta_{D})}
be the inverses to the Kelvin matrices

\{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{j}\}
, it should hold that

\,\overline{\!{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{S}}_{j}}}^{(\vartheta_{D})}=(\,\overline{\!{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{j}}}^{(\vartheta_{D})})^{-1}
.

Desiderata for a metric to define a Fréchet mean:

When looking at the variational definition of the Fréchet mean
Eq.(
45
), but with another metric
\vartheta_{D}
in place of the Euclidean
metric, or the more general formulas in
Appendix
A
, it is quite obvious that the Fréchet mean inherits the
required properties from the underlying metric
\vartheta_{D}
on
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
.
The requirements for a desired metric
\vartheta_{D}
, and hence for a Fréchet mean based on it,
are thus to satisfy for all
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{2}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T}),\alpha>0
,
and all
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
:

1.

quasi-invariance under scaling:
\vartheta_{D}(\alpha\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{1},\alpha\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{2})=f(\alpha)\vartheta_{D}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{2})
,

with a monotone
f(\alpha)>0
;

2.

invariance under orthogonal transformations:
\vartheta_{D}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{2}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}})=\vartheta_{D}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{2})
;

3.

invariance under inversion:
\vartheta_{D}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{1}^{−1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{2}^{−1})=\vartheta_{D}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{2})
;

The Euclidean distance
\vartheta_{2}
satisfies only the first two items,
and so the arithmetic or Euclidean mean only satisfies the first two requirements for means.
The elasticity metric
\vartheta_{E}
proposed here, see Appendix
A
,
satisfies all three requirements, and so does the associated mean. There are some other
metrics which satisfy all three requirements, see the discussion in

[
48
,
160
]
,
but the elasticity metric seems to deal particularly well with the afore mentioned
swelling, fattening, and shrinking effects.

3.4
Generation of Random Kelvin Matrices

At first assume that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{G}
is fixed, i.e. consider
the generation of random Kelvin matrices at one location. The spatial modelling
will be described later, so the argument
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}
will be suppressed.
Similarly to the situation in Section
3.1

when introducing
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}=\log\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
, where a ‘reference matrix’
\widehat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})

was used in Eq.(
41
), this concept is useful also here.
One may actually think of the
reference matrix
\widehat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
as an appropriate mean or expected value.
It will be pointed out under what conditions this holds. Start with the Lie product group
decomposition of the deterministic reference matrix
\widehat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
as in Eq.(
36
):

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{0}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{0}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{0},\quad\text{ with }\quad(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{0},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0})=(\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}_{0},\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{0}),

(48)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}\ni(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}_{0},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{0},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{0})

\displaystyle\mapsto(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{0},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{0})\mapsto\widehat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}}:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{0}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{0}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{0}=\log\widehat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}\in\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T});

(49)

from where
\widehat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}=\exp\widehat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{H}}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
can be recovered as in
Eq.(
44
). Here one can then choose an elasticity invariance class
according what is appropriate for the mean as indicated in Section
2.3
,
i.e. invariance under
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}},m}
resp.
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}},m}
.

How to pick a specific elasticity invariance class either for the mean — i.e.
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}},m}

resp.
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}},m}
— or for the random part — i.e.
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}}
resp.
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
— will
be given in detail for each class in the next Section
4
.

As a second step, in Eq.(
44
) each component
(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}})\in\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}

is written as a product

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{1}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{0},\quad

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{1}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\exp(\theta(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}_{1}(\omega));

(50)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0},\quad

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{1}(\omega));

(51)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{1}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{0},\quad

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{1}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{1}(\omega)),

(52)

computed from random elements
(\theta(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}_{1}(\omega),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{1}(\omega),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{1}(\omega))\in\mathfrak{r}
. The random
variable equivalent to Eq.(
36
) resp. Eqs.(
43
) and (
44
)
then is

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\omega)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}(\omega)\\
=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{1}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{0})^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0})(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{0}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{1}(\omega))(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0})^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{1}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{0}),

(53)

where
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{0}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0})\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{1}(\omega)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}(\omega))\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})

originate from spatial rotations
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}(\omega)\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
.

Random rotations in physical space
\mathcal{V}
:

The purpose of the rotation in Eq.(
50
),
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}(\omega)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}})\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
,
resulting from a spatial rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
,
is to transforms the desired final
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
to
the special form
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
— that is why in Eq.(
53
)
its inverse
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{-1}(\omega)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}(\omega)
is used for the back-transformation.
The special forms to be addressed below for each elasticity class have
at least one distinguished rotation axis — except for tri-clinic material, which has
none, and isotropic material, which does not need a spatial rotation —
which as is usual taken to be the 3-axis.
Additionally, there may be other distinguished rotation axes in the plane orthogonal to
the 3-axis, i.e. the 1-2-plane. In
\mathrm{O}(\mathcal{V})
there also would be reflections,
but as here we confine ourselves to proper rotations
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
, reflections are
not considered any further.

As recalled in Appendix
B.2
, a rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
can be
represented by a normalised skew matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}\in\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{V})
and a rotation angle

\theta
through the exponential map given by the Rodrigues formulas,
Eq.(
104
) in 3D and Eq.(
107
) in 2D.

In 2D the skew matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}
is constant, cf. Eq.(
106
), whereas in 3D
it is conveniently specified by a vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=\theta\,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}
. Here

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}
is a unit vector, and the length of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}
is equal to the absolute value of
\theta
,
and from that one obtains
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}})
(cf. Eq.(
103
)).

In 2D one may take the parameter
q=\theta
(a 1D vector), and in 3D the
3D-vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
(
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=\theta\,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}
).
Thus the deterministic part
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0}
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}(\omega)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0}

is always described by a deterministic vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{Q}}
,
m_{Q}\leq\dim\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{V})

(so in 2D
\dim\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{V})=1
and in 3D
\dim\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{V})=3
),
and the random part
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}(\omega)
through a random vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{Q}}
.
Finally one obtains a correspondence for Eq.(
53
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{m_{Q}}\ni\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{0}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{0}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0})\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T}),

(54)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{m_{Q}}\ni\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\omega)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{1}(\omega)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}(\omega))\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T}),

(55)

check Appendix
B.3
for further details.

One way to think of this combined rotation — a concept which is employed also for the
factors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\omega)
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\omega)
in Eq.(
53
) — is that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{0}\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})

does the ‘main part’, i.e. it transforms the block diagonal form
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}

into the average spatial orientation. The additional factor

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{1}(\omega)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}(\omega))\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
introduces a random component on top of this.
This will be achieved if the expected value of this random rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}(\omega)
— for the

\vartheta_{R}
-metric on
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
in Eq.(
89
) — is the identity.
The total rotation then has mean
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0}
. Thus, in light of Eqs.(
54
) and (
55
),
one may in effect say that the random orientation can be written as

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}(\omega)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\omega))\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{0}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{0})
.

The identity
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}=\exp(\theta\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}})
corresponds to
\theta=0
, i.e. to
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}
.
Thus, for a random realisation, generate a random
spatial rotation vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
with mean at the origin.
Note that from Eqs.(
89
) and (
50
) one gleans that the
\vartheta_{R}
-distance
(cf. Appendix
A
) between
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0}
is

\vartheta_{R}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0})=\left\|\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{0}^{-1})\right\|_{F}=\left\|\log\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}(\omega)\right\|_{F}=\left\|\theta(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}_{1}(\omega)\right\|_{F}.

(56)

As this shows,
one possibility when this will make the corresponding
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}(\omega)
have mean
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}

in the
\vartheta_{R}
metric is when
\theta(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}_{1}(\omega)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\omega))

resp.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\omega)
has a distribution which is symmetric about the origin.
Observe that if in addition to the mean (the origin) also the variance of the
vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{Q}}
is specified, the
maximum entropy distribution

(e.g.
[
148
]
) for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\omega)
is the centred normal or
Gaussian distribution, which obviously is symmetric to its mean, the origin.

Random transformations in strain space
\mathcal{T}
:

This is in many ways very similar to random rotations in physical space as just described,
only that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\omega)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}
with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1}(\omega)=\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{1}(\omega))
in Eq.(
51
)
is in
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
, and the randomness enters here in a slightly different way.

As a general spatial rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}})\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
with

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
can not diagonalise the Kelvin matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
for any elasticity
invariance class, a further rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
in strain representer
space
\mathcal{T}
is needed for this. Thus these orthogonal
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{0},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1}(\omega)=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{1}(\omega)
,
described by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{0},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{1}(\omega)\in\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})^{\perp}\subset\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})
,
are part of the material description, i.e. the definition of the block diagonal
group-reduced form of the Kelvin matrix
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
, as its columns specify
the Kelvin eigen-strain representers resp. the eigenvectors of the elasticity matrix
in the invariant subspace that was found through the reduction of the group representation.
So, although it is an abstract rotation one is talking about, this is not a rotation
in physical space.

One chooses again, analogous as before for the spatial rotation, a deterministic

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{0}\in\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})^{\perp}\subset\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})
to define the reference
or mean eigen-strain, again defined by a parameter vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{V}}
,
where
m_{V}\leq\dim\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})^{\perp}
,
and a random
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{1}(\omega)\in\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})^{\perp}
, also defined by a parameter
vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{V}}
.
As previously for the spatial rotation in Eqs.(
54
) and (
55
), one obtains

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{m_{V}}\ni\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{0}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{0})\in\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}_{0}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T}),

(57)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{m_{V}}\ni\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\omega)

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{1}(\omega)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\omega))\in\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1}(\omega)=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}_{1}(\omega)\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T}).

(58)

Thus again the final result may be written as
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\omega)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\omega))\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{0})
.
Details for these versions of the map
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}
, as well as
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}
will be
given for each elasticity class in Section
4
.

In case
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\omega)
has a
distribution which is symmetric about the origin, and as for spatial rotations the

\vartheta_{R}
-distance (cf. Appendix
A
) is used on the
Lie product factor
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
, similar considerations as before show that then

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1}(\omega)
has the
\vartheta_{R}
-mean equal to the identity
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}
and thus

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}
is the
\vartheta_{R}
-mean of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\omega)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1}(\omega)
.
And again, in case that additionally also the variance of the
vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{V}}
is specified, the
maximum entropy distribution

(e.g.
[
148
]
) for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\omega)
is the centred normal or
Gaussian distribution.

Random Kelvin moduli:

The diagonal eigenvalue matrix we want to split according to Eq.(
52
), similarly as
for the spatial rotations and the rotations in strain space
\mathcal{T}
, i.e.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\omega)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{1}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{0}\in\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
. One may think of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{0}
as
the deterministic or mean part, and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{1}(\omega)
as random with mean equal to unity.
Translating this into logarithmic space, one obtains a modelling on
\mathfrak{diag}(\mathcal{T})
by
taking
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{0}\in\mathfrak{diag}(\mathcal{T})
as fixed such that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{0}=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{0}
and

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{1}(\omega)\in\mathfrak{diag}(\mathcal{T})
as a zero mean random part such that

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\omega)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{1}(\omega)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{0}=(\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{1}(\omega))(\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{0})=\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{1}(\omega)+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{0}),

(59)

the last equality being true as
\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
is an Abelian Lie group, and therefore

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{0}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{1}
commute.

One may point out that in case the distribution of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{1}(\omega)
is symmetric to
the origin, then the expected value of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{1}(\omega)
according to the logarithmic distance

\vartheta_{L}
(see Eq.(
90
) in Appendix
A
) will be the unit matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}
.
And additionally it may be observed that the
maximum entropy distribution

for given mean and variance on
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{1}(\omega)
is the centred
the Gaussian or normal distribution.

To obtain a concise representation in real parameters, one chooses a deterministic
vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0}=[\mu_{0,1},\dots,\mu_{0,m_{\Lambda}}]\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{\Lambda}}
,
where
m_{\Lambda}
is the number of different log-eigenvalues,
of the diagonal of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{0}=\diag_{\#}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0}
, and the subscript # means that the
entries of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0}
are distributed at the right positions of the diagonal.
Additionally, the zero-mean random part
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{1}(\omega)
is parametrised by
a random vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}(\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{\Lambda}}
, which is then used in

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{1}(\omega)=\diag_{\#}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}(\omega)
.
Finally one obtains (cf. Eq. (
59
))

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}(\omega))+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{0}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0});\;\text{ and }

(60)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}(\omega))\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{0}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0})=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}(\omega)=\diag_{\#}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{e}}{\textstyle\mathrm{e}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{e}}^{(\mu_{1,k}(\omega)+\mu_{0,k})}).

(61)

Sometimes one wants a guarantee that for each realisation
\omega\in\Omega
one has

\lambda_{1,k}(\omega)>\lambda_{1,k+1}(\omega)
.
This can be achieved by not generating
\lambda_{1,k}
and
\lambda_{1,k+1}
through their logarithms
as just explained, but rather to realise that
\lambda_{1,k}=\rho_{k}+\lambda_{1,k+1}
, where

\rho_{k}
has to be positive. So one generates the free variables
\mu_{1,k+1}
— the logarithm
of
\lambda_{1,k+1}
— and
\tau_{k}
— the logarithm of the difference

\lambda_{1,k}-\lambda_{1,k+1}
— and sets
\lambda_{1,k+1}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{e}}{\textstyle\mathrm{e}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{e}}^{\mu_{1,k+1}}
,

\rho_{k}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{e}}{\textstyle\mathrm{e}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{e}}^{\tau_{k}}
, and then
\lambda_{1,k}=\lambda_{1,k+1}+\rho_{k}
.
As in every realisation one has that

\rho_{k}>0
, this guarantees that
\lambda_{1,k}(\omega)>\lambda_{1,k+1}(\omega)
for
each realisation
\omega\in\Omega
.

Probability distributions of the parameters:

As was described in the previous sections, the random parameters
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}

determine the stochastic behaviour of the generated Kelvin matrices
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
. Therefore it should be
mentioned what influence the character of the probability distributions for those random
parameters has on the solvability of e.g. the elliptic boundary value problem.

In the normal
\mathrm{H}^{1}-\mathrm{L}_{2}
setting, as will be described in a bit more detail further
below, the Kelvin matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
and its inverse has to be essentially bounded independent
of spatial position or random event for the boundary value problem to be well-posed.

The parameters
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}
are mapped through the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{V})

resp.
\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})
into the compact Lie groups
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
resp.
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
,
and thus have no influence on the question of boundedness.
If their distributions are unbounded, they are wrapped on the compact Lie group to
produce so-called circular statistics, e.g. see
[
84
]

— just think of the 2D case, where the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(2)
is the real line, which
is mapped via
\alpha\mapsto(\cos(\alpha),\sin(\alpha))
onto the circle and
in this way onto the corresponding rotation matrix.
Thus the mapped probability distributions of the rotation matrices
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}

are always bounded, and hence can not cause the Kelvin matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
or
its inverse to be unbounded.

This is different for the eigenvalues, the Kelvin moduli or their logarithms
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}
.
In case the distribution of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}
is bounded, so are the distributions of

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
or its inverse. But in case it is only known that the
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}
have finite
variance, the maximum entropy distribution
[
85
]
is
Gaussian, and hence unbounded. Thus it is also unbounded for
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
or
its inverse. Further below this will be discussed further.

In Section
2.4
it was advocated to model random fields in a separated fashion like
in Eq.(
30
), as this separates the probabilistic content from the spatial variation
in a tensor-product like manner. Given the spatial functions, the attached random variables
will be given through some kind of projection like Eq.(
32
). One possibility
how to express these random variables through a polynomial chaos expansion in
