independent standard Gaussian random variables was already
sketched at the end of Section
2.4
. This can be taken as one example of the so-called
NORTA (NORmal To Anything) approach, cf. e.g.
[
24
,
55
]
, where
groups of random variables with known correlation and marginal distributions are determined
as functions of standard normal or Gaussian random variables.

Especially in the modelling of the Kelvin moduli, observe that what is proposed is to model
the log-moduli, and then essentially take the exponential, thereby guaranteeing positivity.
This means that the point-wise exponential transforms the probability distribution of the
log-moduli into the one of the Kelvin moduli themselves. If, as a realistic example, the
log-moduli
\mu
were modelled as a Gaussian field, the Kelvin moduli
\lambda=\exp\mu

would be log-normal. One may interpret the exponential here also in a different way: Let

F_{g}
be the Gaussian cumulative distribution function of the log-moduli
\mu
, and
F_{\ell}
the
log-normal cumulative distribution function of the Kelvin moduli
\lambda
,
then
F_{\ell}^{-1}\circ F_{g}=\exp
. Now it easy to see, that if some other probability distribution
of the Kelvin moduli
\lambda
were desired, say a probability distribution with
cumulative distribution function
F_{o}
, then one could simply take the point transformation

\lambda=F_{o}^{-1}\circ F_{g}(\mu)
. The thing to watch out for in this case is not to
make any approximations for
F_{o}^{-1}\circ F_{g}
which could numerically destroy positivity.

Spatial random parametrisation:

Putting everything together, one may now define with
n=m_{Q}+m_{V}+m_{\Lambda}
a
parameter vector space
\mathbb{R}^{n}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{m_{Q}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{V}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{\Lambda}}
and
in it a deterministic vector
\,\overline{\!{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}}}:=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{0},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{0},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0})
to define the deterministic
(cf. Eqs.(
43
) and (
44
)) part

(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}_{0}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{0}),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{0}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{0}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0}))\in\mathfrak{r}
, and also the zero mean random vector

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}(\omega):=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\omega),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\omega),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}(\omega)),\quad\text{ to define }

(62)

\displaystyle(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\omega)),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\omega)),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}(\omega)))\in\mathfrak{r}.

(63)

Now we set
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}(\omega):=\,\overline{\!{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}}}+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}(\omega)
, and following
Eqs.(
50
)–(
53
)
one thus arrives at the construction of the desired random Kelvin matrices

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\omega)=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}(\omega))
at any spatial point
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{G}
.

To extend this to the construction of random spatial fields of Kelvin matrices, one may
recall Section
2.4
. Thus to extend the above modelling to random fields,
one observes that the above description of a parameter vector has to be extended to spatial
parameter vector fields. Thus, by defining a deterministic spatial field

\,\overline{\!{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{0}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{0}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}))\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathcal{G}
, and a zero mean random field

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{z}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega))\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, one has
everything that is needed to define random fields of Kelvin matrices with all the desired
properties.

The random parameter field
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
is up to stochastic second order described by its
mean
\,\overline{\!{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
and covariance
\text{cov}_{z}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
see Eqs.(
27
) and (
29
) in Section
2.4
.
The covariance now takes the more detailed form of a joint correlation matrix function:

\text{cov}_{z}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})=\mathbb{E}\left((\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot))\otimes(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}},\cdot),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}},\cdot),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}},\cdot))\right)\\
=\mathbb{E}\left(\begin{matrix}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot)\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}},\cdot)&\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot)\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}},\cdot)&\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot)\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}},\cdot)\\
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot)\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}},\cdot)&\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot)\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}},\cdot)&\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot)\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}},\cdot)\\
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot)\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}},\cdot)&\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot)\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}},\cdot)&\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\cdot)\otimes\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}},\cdot)\end{matrix}\right).

(64)

This form shows that it contains all the correlation structures between spatial
orientation, encoded in
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}
,
eigen-strain distribution, encoded in
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}
, and stiffness, encoded in
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}
.

Heterogeneous materials and their statistical description
may be found in
[
182
]
and references therein.
The family of Matérn covariance functions
(e.g.
[
104
,
34
,
58
]
) are an often used family
of covariance functions in this connection. They allow a separate control of
smoothness and correlation length of the random field, as well as
efficient approximation via low-rank tensor methods
[
93
]
, which
is important in the solution of the Fredholm integral equation Eq.(
31
)
and field expansion Eq.(
30
).

This kind of covariance function is used to describe stochastically homogeneous random
fields as already described at the end of Section
2.4
, where the Karhunen-Loève -eigenproblem
Eq.(
31
) becomes a convolution equation. This means that the — possibly generalised
— eigenfunctions are the Fourier functions. Given this, the spectrum of the operator
in Eq.(
31
) is computed via Fourier transform of the covariance function,
cf.
[
108
]
.
In addition to the versatility of this family of covariance functions this (continuous) spectrum
in the eigenproblem Eq.(
31
) is analytically known.

Unfortunately, neither this covariance nor its
spectrum are compactly supported — an indication that the integral operator in
Eq.(
31
) is not compact. One example of a covariance function with compact support is
given in
[
57
]
. This renders the integral operator in Eq.(
31
)
compact, hence it has a discrete spectrum and a proper Karhunen-Loève series expansion
Eq.(
30
) — whereas in the case of a continuous spectrum this becomes an integral.

Boundary value problems:

Let us return to the question of regularity, resp. boundedness required from the
field of Kelvin matrices
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
. The solution space for an elasticity boundary
value problem with a deterministic field
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
as coefficients for the elastic displacement
field
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
would in the simplest instance be a closed subspace of the Hilbert-Sobolev
space
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathcal{G})
, incorporating the essential boundary conditions,
say for simplicity
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})\in\mathring{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(\mathcal{G})
, i.e.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})

vanishes on the boundary
\partial\mathcal{G}
. The right-hand side of distributed loads
then typically has to be in the dual space
\mathrm{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{G})
.
Thus the Kelvin matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
as coefficient field has to be in
\mathrm{L}_{\infty}(\mathcal{G})
,
i.e. essentially bounded, in order to have a well-posed problem,
e.g.
[
25
,
108
,
134
,
135
,
26
]
.
For that, one would also want the inverse of the Kelvin matrix,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}^{-1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
,
to be essentially bounded. This is well known standard elliptic theory,
e.g.
[
25
,
159
,
186
,
134
,
135
,
26
]
.

Considering the stochastic extension of the boundary value problem with a random
Kelvin matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
, one approach is to require finite second moments
for the random solution displacement field
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{u}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
, i.e. to
be in the tensor product Hilbert space
\mathring{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(\mathcal{G})\otimes\mathrm{L}_{2}(\Omega)
,
and the right-hand side of random distributed loads to be in its dual space

\mathrm{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{G})\otimes\mathrm{L}_{2}(\Omega)
.
The formulation of a well-posed problem in this case requires
the random Kelvin matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)\in\mathrm{L}_{\infty}(\mathcal{G}\times\Omega)
to be essentially bounded, as well as its inverse,
e.g. see
[
106
,
163
,
8
,
108
,
105
,
94
,
35
]
.

This may be regarded as the standard case, and, as mentioned above, it requires the
probability densities of the log-eigenvalues
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}
to be essentially bounded.
One possibility is the quite versatile beta distribution, see e.g.
[
60
]
.
But, as already mentioned, the maximum entropy distribution for the
log-eigenvalues
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}
with known finite second moments is the normal
or Gaussian distribution
[
85
]
, making this a highly desired choice

[
158
]
.
In this case the eigenvalues and thus the Kelvin matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\omega)
have a
lognormal distribution and are thus unbounded. It is possible
to proceed also in this case, with different techniques, which either make the solution
space smaller, or change the solution concept slightly; see e.g.
[
45
,
94
,
78
,
9
,
10
,
77
,
35
]

for more details.

The numerical discretisation and solution of such stochastic boundary value problems resp. stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs) is beyond the scope of this paper,
and the interested reader
may find more material in e.g.
[
106
,
8
,
108
,
105
,
59
,
45
,
78
,
94
,
77
,
35
]
and the references therein.
The numerical solution of such SPDEs is
also necessary when one wants to identify the properties of the tensor field from measurements
resp. observations.

Identification of the parameters:

The tasks of identification, calibration, and conditioning have already been briefly
touched upon at the end of Section
2.4
. Mathematically speaking, these are all
so-called inverse problems. In our view, general techniques for such identification
resp. inverse problems are the Bayesian methods, see e.g.
[
123
,
172
,
102
,
103
,
170
,
152
,
144
,
151
,
107
,
112
,
150
,
109
]

and the references therein. These probabilistic methods may be used for many purposes,
e.g. for anisotropic materials
[
49
,
156
]
. But the detailed description
of the
identification of the symmetry classes of materials and of the numerical values
of the properties, i.e. the eigenvalues and orientations, is a topic beyond the scope
of this work, so we offer only some remarks.
The identification of symmetry classes may be based e.g. on the knowledge of the
crystal structure of the material
[
133
]
, or on its fabrication

[
27
]
, or of its natural genesis.
Otherwise one must rely on measurements or observations. For the identification
of elasticity classes, see
[
29
,
53
,
36
,
37
,
56
]
.
For the kind of stochastic modelling proposed here, some pointers are given in
section 4.2 of
[
60
]
.

With informative measurements or tests
quite complicated material behaviour can be identified and calibrated, see e.g.
[
151
,
107
,
112
,
150
]
, and also e.g.
[
42
,
79
,
80
]

for highly nonlinear and irreversible behaviour.
Whether the tests or experiments are informative may be tested computationally beforehand.
Thus the test or measurement and updating may be performed first virtually in the computer,
and in this way help in the design of informative experiments.

4
Detailed Lie Representation

According to Eq.(
62
) in Section
3.4
, the random field of Kelvin
matrices can be described by a
n
-dimensional random field of parameters
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
where
n=m_{Q}+m_{V}+m_{\Lambda}
, and

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{z}}=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})
is such that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{Q}}
describes the
spatial rotations,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{V}}
describes the eigen-strain distribution, and

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{\Lambda}}
describes the eigenvalues or Kelvin moduli.
These parameters will now be given for all the elasticity classes. As this is simpler in 2D,
this is where we start in Section
4.1
, and then move to the 3D case in Section
4.2
.
As we will accept possible ‘wrapping’ in the parametrisation of rotations, for the sake of
simplicity it is refrained from giving the formal group-theoretic descriptions of the
material symmetry resp. invariance groups. This will be described only verbally by
pointing out familiar geometrical objects with the same symmetry.

Here the special form of the Kelvin matrix
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
(see the development
Eqs.(
17
) and (
19
) in Section
2.3
)
will be given in its group reduced form, i.e. reduced to block-diagonal form.
In cases where one can not achieve two or more diagonal blocks — e.g. for
tri-clinic materials — one at least wants to have a form which has as many
zeros in
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
as possible.
The special form
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
can then be spatially rotated — the reverse of
Eq.(
17
) — to the final form
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}
,
this is all contained in the relations Eq.(
35
) at the beginning of Section
3
.
How to build the orthogonal
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
from a spatial rotation

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
is recalled for the sake of completeness from the literature
in Appendix
B
.

4.1
Elasticity classes in 2D

In 2D there are four symmetry classes
[
15
]
, to be shortly and informally described
by pointing out a geometric figure with the same symmetry. The Hasse diagram — drawn
horizontally in Fig.
1
— shows the relation of the lattice of elasticity symmetry or
invariance groups as subgroups of the full orthogonal group, where the hooked arrow means
a subgroup relation, e.g. the symmetry group of the orthotropic class is a subgroup of the
symmetry group of the tetragonal class. The largest group is on the left,
the
isotropic
class with the symmetries of a circle (i.e. the whole 2D rotation group),
then the next smaller one is the
tetragonal
class with the symmetry of a square, then
the
orthotropic
class with the symmetry of a general rectangle, and finally the

tri-clinic
class with the symmetry of a parallelogram.

tri
[isotropic]
[tetragonal]
[orthotropic]
[tri-clinic]

Figure 1
:
Hasse diagram of symmetry subgroups of 4th order tensors in 2D

Tri-clinic material in 2D:

This section covers not only tri-clinic, but also mono-clinic materials, as there is no
difference in 2D.
As mentioned before, these materials have in 2D the symmetry of a parallelogram.
The Kelvin matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
can not be simplified through a group reduction with a
spatial rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})

to a block-diagonal form beyond the general form Eq.(
13
), where
one may note that there are
n=6=\dim\,\mathcal{E}=\dim\,\mathcal{S}
free parameters.
But, as in 2D one has
\dim\,\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})=\dim\,\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})=1
, one parameter

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=(\theta)\in\mathcal{Q}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{m_{Q}}
(
m_{Q}=1
) may be used
for a spatial rotation, see Eqs.(
117
) and (
120
) in
Appendix
B
, to induce one zero in the upper right corner
[
31
]
,
this echos the first of the relations in Eq.(
35
):

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\mathrm{tri-2D}}={\small\begin{bmatrix}\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}&0\\
&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2222}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2212}\\
\mathrm{SYM}&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1212}\end{bmatrix}}.

(65)

The spatial rotations
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
resp.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})

decide on whether the spatial orientation of the material axes changes or not.
Hence one wants to keep this possibility in the stochastic modelling process.

What is needed next is to use the spectral decomposition of
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
, the second of the
relations in Eq.(
35
).
The matrix in Eq.(
65
) has generally three distinct positive eigenvalues

\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
, and
\lambda_{3}
.
For the three distinct eigenvalues one thus needs
m_{\Lambda}=3
log-eigenvalue
parameters
\mathbb{R}^{m_{\Lambda}}\ni\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3})\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=\diag(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})
, such that

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})=\diag(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}
.

The Kelvin matrix
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
in Eq.(
65
) can of course be diagonalised
as in Eq.(
18
), but the corresponding matrix of eigenvectors

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
is not in
\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
, i.e. not induced by a spatial rotation.

In Section
3.1
it was explained how to keep the spatial rotation
described by
\mathcal{Q}\ni\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}
— cf. Appendix
B.3

— from interfering with the ‘strain distributor’ part
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})

by introducing the orthogonal split in the corresponding Lie algebra
already mentioned in Eq.(
42
):
\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})=\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})\oplus\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})^{\perp}
.
As just mentioned, in 2D one has
\dim\,\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})=1=m_{Q}
, and this is needed for the rotation
angle parameter
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=(\theta)\in\mathcal{Q}
to describe the spatial rotation.
This means
m_{V}=n-m_{Q}-m_{\Lambda}=6-1-3=2=\dim\,\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})^{\perp}
angle-like
parameters
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}=(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathcal{Q}^{\perp}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{m_{V}}
can now be used to describe the
eigenvectors of the Kelvin matrix in Eq.(
65
), i.e. the columns
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
in Eq.(
18
).

There are at least two ways of generating such a
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
: one is to use the
Eqs.(
121
)–(
122
) in Appendix
B.3
, where the
parameters
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\perp}
are directly used for a map

\mathcal{Q}^{\perp}\to\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})^{\perp}\to\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
to generate a

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(s_{1},s_{2})\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
.
Another way is to use two 2D rotation matrices
— cf. Eq.(
105
) — embedded in 3D depending on angles
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}=(s_{1},s_{2})
:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{i}(s_{1}):=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&\cos s_{1}&-\sin s_{1}\\
0&\sin s_{1}&\phantom{-}\cos s_{1}\end{bmatrix};\qquad\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{ii}(s_{2}):=\begin{bmatrix}\cos s_{2}&-\sin s_{2}&0\\
\sin s_{2}&\phantom{-}\cos s_{2}&0\\
0&0&1\end{bmatrix}.

(66)

Here the explicit formulation of the skew matrices
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{i},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}_{ii}\in\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})
and the
explicit use of the exponential map in the mapping chain
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}

according to Appendix
B.2
has been shortened by giving just the final result.
Then with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(s_{1},s_{2})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{i}(s_{1})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{ii}(s_{2})
it is easily seen
that (cf. Eq.(
18
))

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})

(67)

has the form Eq.(
65
), which corresponds to the spectral decomposition as the
second of the relations in Eq.(
35
). In both cases the parameters
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}=(s_{1},s_{2})

are purely a description of the distribution of eigen-strains for the material specification.
Note that the first column of the matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1}
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}=[\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{3}]
is the
eigenvector or eigen-strain for the eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}
,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2}
the eigenvector of
\lambda_{2}
,
and so on. If one wants to distinguish the bulk or volumetric response from the shear response,
e.g. by assigning the largest of the eigenvalues to this response, then one may
choose the eigenvector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{j}
which has the largest projection onto the volumetric strain

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}
(Eq.(
132
) in Appendix
C.1
). By assigning the largest eigenvalue
to that eigenvector — it was described previously how to generate random eigenvalues where one
is always larger than the others — one can ascertain that the bulk or volumetric response
is always stiffer than the shear response.

By construction, the form in Eq.(
67
) is invariant under rotations
in the symmetry group
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}}})=[\text{tri-clinic}]
.
Finally, the elasticity matrix for a tri-clinic material in 2D with the proper spatial
orientation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=(\theta)
may be generated from Eq.(
67
)
in a 2-step rotation procedure via

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{\mathrm{tri-2D}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}})\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}),\quad\text{ or even in more detail }

(68)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}_{0}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{1}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{0}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{0}.

The last equation in Eq.(
68
) — the third of the relations
in Eq.(
35
) — is the concrete version of
Eq.(
53
) in Section
3.4
. One may recall that

\widehat{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}_{0}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{0}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{0}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{0}
is the constant reference matrix —
e.g. the scaling-rotation mean — which may be actually invariant under a larger
symmetry group
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}},m}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathbf{c}}{\textstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptstyle\mathbf{c}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{c}},m})\supset\mathfrak{S}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
, i.e. further to the left
in Fig.
1
.
In case one of the eigenvectors is a pure shear, i.e. proportional to
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}
in Eq.(
133
)
in Appendix
C.1
, then the material is in fact
orthotropic
, to be
described next.

Orthotropic material in 2D:

As already indicated, such materials have the symmetry of a general rectangle in 2D.
By the group reduction process, i.e. a spatial rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
given by one parameter

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=(\theta)\in\mathcal{Q}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{m_{Q}}
(
m_{Q}=1
), see
Eqs.(
117
) and (
120
) in Appendix
B
,
the Kelvin matrix for these materials may be brought into the following block-diagonal
form
[
31
]
, with four free parameters:

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\mathrm{ortho-2D}}={\small\begin{bmatrix}\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}&0\\
&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2222}&0\\
\mathrm{SYM}&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1212}\end{bmatrix}},

(69)

which has one obvious eigen-stiffness or Kelvin shear modulus
\lambda_{3}=2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1212}

with associated eigenvector or eigen-strain distributor

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{3}=[0,0,1]^{{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}},

(70)

a simple shear. The other two come from the diagonalisation of the upper
2\times 2

upper left sub-matrix of Eq.(
69
), and have the general form of a rotation
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}
in Eqs.(
132
) and (
133
)
about the fixed vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{3}
in Eq.(
70
)in the strain-representer space
\mathcal{T}
:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1}(s)=[\cos s,\sin s,0]^{{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2}(s)=[-\sin s,\cos s,0]^{{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}},

(71)

associated with the other two Kelvin moduli
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
.
Observe that the single parameter
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}=(s)\in\mathcal{Q}^{\perp}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{m_{V}}
(
m_{V}=1
)
is not an angle in a spatial sense, but is one in the two-dimensional
strain representer subspace formed by the first two components,
characterising the material, or more precisely, the Kelvin eigen-strains.
Here, as just before, the mapping chain
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}
according to
Appendix
B.2
has been shortened to display just the final result
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})
.

The previous remarks made for tri-clinic material regarding the
possible choice of the largest Kelvin module to
be associated with the volumetric response apply here as well.
Again, for the three generally distinct eigenvalues one needs
m_{\Lambda}=3
log-eigenvalue
parameters
\mathbb{R}^{m_{\Lambda}}\ni\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3})\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=\diag(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})
, such that

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})=\diag(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}
.
Additionally, one has with Eqs.(
70
) and (
71
)

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(s)=[\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1}(s)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2}(s)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{3}]
, and with that
formally the same kind of spectral representation as in Eq.(
67
)
for
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\mathrm{ortho-2D}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})
.
The Kelvin elasticity matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{\mathrm{ortho-2D}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})
for
an orthotropic material in 2D with the proper spatial orientation

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=(\theta)
may then again be generated as in Eq.(
53
) in Section
3.4
,
or in Eq.(
68
)
in a 2-step rotation procedure, see also the remarks there.
The total number of parameters
(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})

is
n=m_{Q}+m_{\Lambda}+m_{V}=1+3+1=5
.
In case
s=\uppi/4
in Eq.(
71
), i.e. in case the first two eigenvectors
are
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}
, the material is actually
tetragonal
.

Tetragonal material in 2D :

As already indicated, such materials have the symmetry of a square in 2D.
Again, by the group reduction process and a spatial rotation given by one parameter

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=(\theta)\in\mathcal{Q}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{m_{Q}}
(
m_{Q}=1
),
the Kelvin matrix for these materials may be brought into the following block-diagonal
form
[
31
]
, with three free parameters:

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\mathrm{tetra-2D}}={\small\begin{bmatrix}\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}&0\\
&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&0\\
\mathrm{SYM}&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1212}\end{bmatrix}},

(72)

completely determining the spectral decomposition, with, as before in the 2D orthotropic case
with the obvious Kelvin shear modulus
\lambda_{3}=2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1212}
and eigenvector

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{3}
as in Eq.(
70
), and the two additional generally distinct
Kelvin moduli (
m_{\Lambda}=3
):
\lambda_{1}=\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}+\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}
and

\lambda_{2}=\mathring{\mathrm{c}_{1111}}-\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}
,
i.e.
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}+2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}
.

The associated eigenvectors are fixed (
m_{V}=0
),
corresponding to
s=\uppi/4
in Eq.(
71
):

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}
, cf. Eqs.(
132
) and (
133
), hence

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}=[\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{3}]
is a fixed matrix.
As is easily observed,
\lambda_{1}
is a volumetric Kelvin modulus,
whereas
\lambda_{2}
is a shear Kelvin modulus; and the previous remarks made for
tri-clinic material regarding the possible choice of the largest Kelvin module to
be associated with the volumetric response apply here as well.

Here again, for the three generally distinct eigenvalues one needs

m_{\Lambda}=3
log-eigenvalue parameters
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3})
. With the above fixed
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}

one obtains formally the same kind of spectral representation as in Eq.(
67
)
for
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\mathrm{tetra-2D}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})
in Eq.(
72
).
The tetragonal 2D Kelvin elasticity matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{\mathrm{tetra-2D}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})

with the proper spatial orientation

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=(\theta)
may then again be generated as in Eq.(
53
) in Section
3.4
,
or in Eq.(
68
)
in a 2-step rotation procedure. The total number of parameters
(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})

is
n=m_{Q}+m_{\Lambda}+m_{V}=1+3+0=4
.
In case the two shear Kelvin moduli coincide, i.e.
\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{3}
,
the material is actually
isotropic
.

Isotropic material in 2D:

As already mentioned, such a material has the symmetry group of a circle in 2D.
This means that no spatial rotation is needed (
m_{Q}=0
) to bring the Kelvin matrix to
the block-diagonal form Eq.(
72
) with
\lambda_{3}=2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1212}=\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}-\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}=\lambda_{2}
,
the Kelvin matrix has this form in any spatial orientation. Thus there are only two
(
n=m_{Q}+m_{\Lambda}+m_{V}=0+2+0=2
) free parameters for the two
log-eigenvalues
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}=\mu_{3}
. The spectral decomposition as in Eq.(
67
)
with the matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}
as in the tetragonal case is the final product

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\mathrm{iso-2D}}
, no spatial rotations
as in Eq.(
53
) in Section
3.4
, or in Eq.(
68
)
are necessary, as the Kelvin matrix
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\mathrm{iso-2D}}
is invariant
under such rotations.

4.2
Elasticity classes in 3D

[isotropic]
[cubic]
[transv. isotropic]
[tetragonal]
[trigonal]
[orthotropic]
[mono-clinic]
[tri-clinic]

Figure 2
:
Hasse diagram of symmetry subgroups of 4th order tensors in 3D

In 3D there are eight elasticity classes
[
31
,
51
,
124
,
161
,
179
,
136
,
7
,
99
,
101
]
. The Hasse diagram for 3D in Fig.
2
shows
the relation of the lattice of elasticity symmetry or invariance groups
as subgroups of the full orthogonal group in 3D. They will be again described
informally by pointing out geometric bodies with the same symmetry. On top is
the
isotropic
class with the symmetries of a sphere,
i.e. the whole orthogonal group, with the sub-classes — descending in the Hasse
diagram in Fig.
2
along the left branch — the
cubic
class
with the symmetries of a cube, the
tetragonal
class
with the symmetries of a square prism (a right prism with a square base), the

orthotropic
class with the symmetries of a right prism (a right prism with a
general rectangle as base), the
mono-clinic
class with the symmetries of
a right prism with a parallelogram as base, and the
tri-clinic
class
with the symmetries of a parallelepiped (an oblique prism with a parallelogram as base).
On the right branch of the Hasse diagram for 3D in Fig.
2
are the

transversely isotropic
class with the symmetries of a circular cylinder and the

trigonal
class with the symmetries of a triangular prism (a right prism with a
equilateral triangle as base).

Tri-clinic material in 3D:

As mentioned before, these materials have in 3D the symmetry of a parallelepiped.
As in the 2D case for tri-clinic materials,
the Kelvin matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
can not be simplified through a group reduction with a
spatial rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}})\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
to a block-diagonal form beyond the general form
Eq.(
12
), and there are
n=21
free parameters.
But, as in 3D one has
\dim\,\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})=\dim\,\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})=3
, three parameters

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\in\mathcal{Q}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{m_{Q}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{15}
(
m_{Q}=3
) may be used
for a spatial rotation, the direction of the vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}
actually being the rotation axis
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}})\in\mathrm{SO}(3)
, see Eqs.(
108
) and (
115
) in
Appendix
B
, to induce three zeros in the right of
the top row
[
31
]
, this echos the first of the relations in Eq.(
35
):

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\text{tri-3D}}={\small\begin{bmatrix}\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1133}&0&0&0\\
&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2222}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2233}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2223}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2213}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2212}\\
&&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{3333}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{3323}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{3313}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{3312}\\
&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2323}&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2313}&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2312}\\
&\mathrm{SYM}&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1313}&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1312}\\
&&&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1212}\end{bmatrix}}.

(73)

As before in 2D, the spatial rotations
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
resp.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})

decide on whether the spatial orientation of the material axes changes or not,
and thus one wants to keep this possibility in the stochastic modelling process.

The matrix in Eq.(
73
) has generally six distinct positive eigenvalues

\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{6}
, for which one needs
m_{\Lambda}=6
log-eigenvalue
parameters to generate the diagonal eigenvalue matrix:

\mathbb{R}^{m_{\Lambda}}\ni\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{6})\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=\diag(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})=\diag(\lambda_{j})=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}
.

Next one wants to use the spectral decomposition of
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\text{tri-3D}}
, the second
of the relations in Eq.(
35
). As a
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
was used to generate the special form
Eq.(
73
), this matrix can be diagonalised
as in Eq.(
18
), but the corresponding matrix of eigenvectors

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
is not induced by a spatial rotation, and thus generated by
a
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\in\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})^{\perp}
. See Section
3.1
on how to keep the spatial rotation

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}
from interfering with the ‘strain distributor’ part
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})

by introducing the orthogonal split in the corresponding Lie algebra
already mentioned in Eq.(
42
):
\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})=\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})\oplus\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})^{\perp}
.
As
\dim\,\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})^{\perp}=m_{V}=n-m_{Q}-m_{\Lambda}=12
, all that is needed now is
a basis of the space
\mathcal{Q}^{\perp}
, given in Eq.(
116
) in Appendix
B.3
.
With that basis
\{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}_{1},\dots,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}_{12}\}
and 12 parameters
(s_{1},\dots,s_{12})

one may form the vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}=\sum_{j=1}^{12}s_{j}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}_{j}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\perp}
, and then map
this on to
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})\in\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})^{\perp}
according to Eq.(
108
).
This is finally mapped to
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})
, to obtain

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\text{tri-3D}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})

as in Eq.(
67
), which corresponds to the spectral decomposition as the
second of the relations in Eq.(
35
).

The columns of the matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}=[\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1},\dots,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{6}]
are the eigenvectors corresponding
to the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{6}
.
As before, in case one wants to distinguish the bulk or volumetric response from the shear
response, e.g. by assigning the largest of the eigenvalues to this response, then one may
choose the eigenvector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{j}
which has the largest projection onto the volumetric strain

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}
given in Eq.(
126
) in Appendix
C.1
, and assign the largest eigenvalue
to that eigenvector.

Finally, as before, the 2-step rotation procedure with a spatial rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}})

given by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}\in\mathcal{Q}
may be used as in Eq.(
68
)
to rotate the Kelvin matrix to the desired coordinate system.
As pointed out before, the last equation in Eq.(
68
) giving

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{\text{tri-3D}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})
here — the third relation in Eq.(
35
) —
is the concrete version of Eq.(
53
) in Section
3.4
, cf. the
remarks for tri-clinic materials in 2D in Section
4.1
.
In case two of the eigenvectors are pure shears, i.e. proportional to

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}
in Eq.(
127
) and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}
in Eq.(
128
) in Appendix
C.1
,
or some linear combination thereof, then the material is in fact
mono-clinic
, to be
described next.

Mono-clinic material in 3D:

As indicated previously, these materials have in 3D the symmetry of a right prism
with a parallelogram as base.
In this case
[
31
]
the Kelvin matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
can be simplified through
a group reduction with a spatial rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
to a block-diagonal form
with three diagonal blocks Eq.(
74
), and in this form there are
12

free parameters. An additional
m_{Q}=\dim\,\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})=\dim\,\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})=3
