parameters

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\in\mathcal{Q}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{m_{Q}}
are used
for the spatial rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}})
just referenced for the group reduction process,
giving the total number of parameters for mono-clinic material as
n=15
;
see Eqs.(
108
) and (
115
) in Appendix
B
,
this is the first relation in Eq.(
35
):

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\text{mono-3D}}={\small\begin{bmatrix}\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1133}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1123}&0&0\\
&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2222}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2233}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2223}&0&0\\
&&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{3333}&\sqrt{2}\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{3323}&0&0\\
&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2323}&0&0\\
&\mathrm{SYM}&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1313}&0\\
&&&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1212}\end{bmatrix}}.

(74)

The matrix in Eq.(
74
) has generally six distinct positive eigenvalues

\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{6}
, for which one needs, as before,
m_{\Lambda}=6
log-eigenvalue
parameters to generate the diagonal eigenvalue matrix:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{6})\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=\diag(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})=\diag(\lambda_{j})=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}
.
Two eigenvalues are immediately recognisable from Eq.(
74
), namely
the Kelvin moduli

\lambda_{5}=2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1313}
and
\lambda_{6}=2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1212}

with corresponding eigenvectors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{5}=[0,0,0,0,1,0]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{5}=[0,0,0,0,0,1]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
.

The other four eigenvectors in
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}=[\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1},\dots,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{4},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{5},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{6}]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
come
from the diagonalisation of the upper left
4\times 4
block
\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
in
Eq.(
74
). This has a spectral decomposition
\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}=\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}}\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}}\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
with
\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}}\in\mathrm{SO}(4)
. As
\dim\,\mathrm{SO}(4)=\dim\,\mathfrak{so}(4)=6
, one needs
m_{V}=6=n-m_{\Lambda}-m_{Q}
additional parameters

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}=[s_{1},\dots,s_{6}]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\perp}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{m_{V}}
to model this.
Taking the 4-dimensional version of Eq.(
108
) in Appendix
B.3
,
this is mapped to
\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})\in\mathfrak{so}(4)
, and this on to

\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})=\exp\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})\in\mathrm{SO}(4)
, yielding the eigen-strains
of the eigenvalues resp. Kelvin moduli of
\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
.
Each column
\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{4},j=1,\dots,4
in
\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}}=[\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}}_{1},\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}}_{2},\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}}_{3},\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}}_{4}]

is then extended into
\mathbb{R}^{6}
by padding with two zeros:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{j}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})=[\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}}_{j}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}),0,0]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{6},j=1,\dots,4
to
complete
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})
.

From this eigen-strain matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})
and the diagonal
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})
one then
obtains a random mono-clinic Kelvin matrix of the form Eq.(
74
):

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\text{mono-3D}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})
,
as in Eq.(
67
), corresponding to the spectral decomposition as the
second of the relations in Eq.(
35
).
The by now familiar 2-step rotation procedure with a spatial rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}})

given by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}\in\mathcal{Q}
may be used as in Eq.(
68
)
to rotate the Kelvin matrix to the desired coordinate system, and again
the last equation in Eq.(
68
) yielding

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{\text{mono-3D}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})
— the third relation in Eq.(
35
) —
is the concrete version of Eq.(
53
) in Section
3.4
.
In case
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{4}=[0,0,0,1,0,0]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
, the material is in fact
orthotropic
.

Orthotropic material in 3D:

It was already mentioned that these materials have in 3D the symmetry of a right prism
— a prism with a general rectangle as base. As in all the previous cases, the group
reduction process with a rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}))\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
leads to a block-diagonal
form Eq.(
75
) with three rotation axis parameters

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\in\mathcal{Q}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{m_{Q}}
(
m_{Q}=3
),
see Eqs.(
108
) and (
115
) in Appendix
B
.
The resulting Kelvin matrix of the form
[
31
]
:

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\text{ortho-3D}}={\small\begin{bmatrix}\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1133}&0&0&0\\
&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2222}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2233}&0&0&0\\
&&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{3333}&0&0&0\\
&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2323}&0&0\\
&\mathrm{SYM}&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1313}&0\\
&&&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1212}\end{bmatrix}}

(75)

has
m_{\Lambda}+m_{V}=9
free parameters, so that together with the rotation axis

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}
with
m_{Q}=3
parameters, in total there are
n=m_{Q}+m_{\Lambda}+m_{V}=12
parameters.

The matrix in Eq.(
75
) has generally six distinct positive eigenvalues

\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{6}
, for which one needs, as before,
m_{\Lambda}=6
log-eigenvalue
parameters to generate the diagonal eigenvalue matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{6})\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=\diag(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})=\diag(\lambda_{j})=\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}
.
Three eigenvalues are obvious from Eq.(
75
), namely
the Kelvin moduli
\lambda_{4}=2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2323}
,

\lambda_{5}=2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1313}
, and
\lambda_{6}=2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1212}

with corresponding eigenvectors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{4}=[0,0,0,1,0,0]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{5}=[0,0,0,0,1,0]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
,
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{6}=[0,0,0,0,0,1]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
.

The other three eigenvectors come
from the diagonalisation of the upper left
3\times 3
block
\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
in
Eq.(
75
). Its spectral decomposition is
\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}=\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}}\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}}\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
with
\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}}\in\mathrm{SO}(3)
. Therefore,

m_{V}=3=n-m_{\Lambda}-m_{Q}
additional parameters

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}=[s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\perp}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{m_{V}}
to model this.
As
m_{V}=3
, one may actually use Rodrigues original formula
Eqs.(
102
)–(
104
) in Appendix
B.2
, such
that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}
is the rotation vector, but now in the strain representer space
\mathcal{T}
,
and not in physical space
\mathcal{V}.
This produces a rotation matrix

\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})=\exp\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})\in\mathrm{SO}(3)
, yielding the eigen-strains
of the eigenvalues resp. Kelvin moduli of
\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
.
Each column
\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
in

\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}}=[\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}}_{1},\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}}_{2},\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}}_{3}]

is then extended into
\mathbb{R}^{6}
by padding with three zeros:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{j}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})=[\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}}_{j}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}),0,0,0]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{6},j=1,\dots,3
to
complete
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})
.
The familiar 2-step rotation procedure with a spatial rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}})

given by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}\in\mathcal{Q}
may be used as in Eq.(
68
)
to rotate the Kelvin matrix to the desired coordinate system.

Trigonal material in 3D:

It was already mentioned that these materials have in 3D the symmetry of a triangular prism.
As in all the previous cases, the Kelvin matrix can be simplified in the group
reduction process with a rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}))\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
with three
rotation axis parameters
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\in\mathcal{Q}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{m_{Q}}
(
m_{Q}=3
)
— a so-called
natural
coordinate system with the spatial 3-axis equal to the
three-fold rotation axis — into a form
[
31
]
with
n-m_{Q}=6
free parameters
(i.e. the total number of parameters is
n=9
):

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\text{trigon-3D}}={\small\begin{bmatrix}\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1133}&\phantom{-}\sqrt{2}\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1123}&0&0\\
&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1133}&-\sqrt{2}\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1123}&0&0\\
&&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{3333}&0&0&0\\
&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2323}&0&0\\
&\mathrm{SYM}&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2323}&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1123}\\
&&&&&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}-\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}\end{bmatrix}}

(76)

The matrix in Eq.(
76
) has generally
[
171
]
only four distinct
positive eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{4}
, as two of them are degenerate
double eigenvalues. For this one needs
m_{\Lambda}=4
log-eigenvalue
parameters to generate the diagonal eigenvalue matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{4})\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=\diag_{\sharp}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})\mapsto\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})
, where
\diag_{\sharp}

means that the four distinct eigenvalues are put in the right six locations on the diagonal,
doubling included.

Now
m_{V}=n-m_{Q}-m_{\Lambda}=9-3-4=2
more parameters
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}=[s_{1},s_{2}]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}

are needed to fully describe the material via the eigenvector matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})=[\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{3},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{4},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{5},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{6}]
. The two simple eigenvectors for

\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
have the general form
[
171
]
:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}[\cos\alpha,\cos\alpha,\sqrt{2}\sin\alpha,0,0,0]^{{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}},\quad\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}[\sin\alpha,\sin\alpha,-\sqrt{2}\cos\alpha,0,0,0]^{{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}}.

(77)

The angle
\alpha
in strain representer space signifies a rotation about the shear

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}
in Eq.(
127
), which means that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2}
are rotated images of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}

in Eq.(
126
) and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}
Eq.(
128
). In case
\tan\alpha=\sqrt{2}
, i.e. for the angle
\alpha_{n}=\arctan\sqrt{2}
, the vectors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2}
coincide with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}
.
therefore we set in Eq.(
77
)
\alpha=\alpha_{n}+s_{1}
, so that
s_{1}=0
corresponds
to the special case.

The two two-dimensional eigen-subspaces with repeated eigenvalues
\lambda_{3}=\lambda_{4}

and
\lambda_{5}=\lambda_{6}
have as possible bases
[
171
]
vectors
of the general form — pure shears:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}[\cos s_{2},-\cos s_{2},0,-\sqrt{2}\sin s_{2},0,0]^{{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}},\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{4}=[0,0,0,0,-\sin s_{2},\cos s_{2}]^{{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}},\text{ and }

(78)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{5}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}[\sin s_{2},-\sin s_{2},0,\phantom{-}\sqrt{2}\cos s_{2},0,0]^{{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}},\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{6}=[0,0,0,0,\phantom{-}\cos s_{2},\sin s_{2}]^{{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}}.

(79)

Again the parameter
s_{2}
can be interpreted as an angle, and note that for
s_{2}=0

these eigenvectors all become simple shears.
With this the description of the
n=9
parameters
(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})
is complete.
The last step is the 2-step rotation procedure as before
to rotate the Kelvin matrix to the desired coordinate system.

Tetragonal material in 3D:

As mentioned, these materials have in 3D the symmetry of a square prism.
As in the previous cases, the Kelvin matrix can be simplified by group
reduction with a rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}))\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
with three
rotation axis parameters
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\in\mathcal{Q}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{m_{Q}}
(
m_{Q}=3
)
— such that the spatial 3-axis is equal to the
four-fold rotation axis —
[
31
]
with
n-m_{Q}=6
free parameters
(i.e. the total number of parameters is
n=9
) into the form:

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\text{tetra-3D}}={\small\begin{bmatrix}\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1133}&0&0&0\\
&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1133}&0&0&0\\
&&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{3333}&0&0&0\\
&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2323}&0&0\\
&\mathrm{SYM}&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2323}&0\\
&&&&&2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1212}\end{bmatrix}},

(80)

which has generally
[
171
]
five independent eigenvalues resp. Kelvin moduli.
Obviously
\lambda_{4}=\lambda_{5}=2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2323}
is a double eigenvalue with
the two-dimensional eigen-space spanned by the vectors

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{4}=[0,0,0,1,0,0]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{5}=[0,0,0,0,1,0]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
.
The other obvious simple eigenvalue is
\lambda_{6}=2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1212}
with
eigenvector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{6}=[0,0,0,0,0,1]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
.
Thus one needs
m_{\Lambda}=5
log-eigenvalue
parameters to generate the diagonal eigenvalue matrix:
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{5})\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=\diag_{\sharp}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})\mapsto\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})
, where again
\diag_{\sharp}

means that the five distinct eigenvalues are put in the right six locations on the diagonal.

The other three eigenvectors come
from the diagonalisation of the upper left
3\times 3
block
\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
in
Eq.(
80
). By simple inspection
[
171
]
it can be seen that

\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}}_{3}={\mathchoice{{{{\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{{$\textstyle 1$}}}{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\kern-1.50696pt\hbox{/}}}{\raise-1.29167pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{{$\textstyle\sqrt{2}$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 3.01385pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{\footnotesize/}}}{\raise-0.86108pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\sqrt{2}$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 3.22916pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.73193pt\hbox{\kern-1.93747pt\hbox{\scriptsize/}}}{\raise-0.6458pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle\sqrt{2}$}}}}}}}{{{{\raise 3.22916pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}{\raise 0.73193pt\hbox{\kern-1.93747pt\hbox{\tiny/}}}{\raise-0.6458pt\hbox{\kern-1.72218pt\hbox{{$\scriptscriptstyle\sqrt{2}$}}}}}}}}[-1,1,0]=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}
(cf. Eq.(
133
)) is a fixed
eigenvector of
\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
with
eigenvalue
\lambda_{3}=\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}-\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}
. Padding

\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}}_{3}
with 3 zeros in the by now familiar fashion gives the fixed eigenvector

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{3}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}
(cf. Eq.(
127
)) of
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\text{tetra-3D}}
in Eq.(
80
).
The other eigenvectors for the two simple eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
of

\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
depend on an angle-like strain-distribution parameter
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}=[\alpha]
,
and have the general form
[
171
]
(already padded with zeros):

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}[\cos\alpha,\cos\alpha,\sqrt{2}\sin\alpha,0,0,0]^{{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}},\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}[\sin\alpha,\sin\alpha,-\sqrt{2}\cos\alpha,0,0,0]^{{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}}.

(81)

This then finally gives the eigenvector matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})=[\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{3},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{4},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{5},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{6}]
.
Thus a tetragonal material including its orientation in the physical space
\mathcal{V}

is completely described by the
n=9
parameters
(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})
.
Finally, the 2-step rotation procedure rotates the Kelvin matrix to the desired coordinate system.

Cubic material in 3D:

As mentioned, these materials have in 3D the symmetry of a cube.
The Kelvin matrix can be simplified by the group
reduction with a rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}))\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
with three
rotation axis parameters
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\in\mathcal{Q}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{m_{Q}}
(
m_{Q}=3
)
— such that the sides of the cube align with the axes —

[
31
]
with
n-m_{Q}=3
free parameters
(i.e. the total number of parameters is
n=6
) into the form:

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\text{cubic-3D}}={\small\begin{bmatrix}\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}&0&0&0\\
&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}&0&0&0\\
&&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&0&0&0\\
&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2323}&0&0\\
&\mathrm{SYM}&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2323}&0\\
&&&&&2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2323}\end{bmatrix}}.

(82)

It has generally
[
171
]
only three independent eigenvalues resp. Kelvin moduli,
one repeated eigenvalue of multiplicity two, and one repeated eigenvalue of multiplicity three,
as
\lambda_{4}=\lambda_{5}=\lambda_{6}=2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2323}
is a triple eigenvalue with
the three-dimensional eigen-space spanned by the vectors

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{4}=[0,0,0,1,0,0]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{5}=[0,0,0,0,1,0]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
, and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{6}=[0,0,0,0,0,1]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
.
Thus one needs
m_{\Lambda}=3
log-eigenvalue
parameters to generate the diagonal eigenvalue matrix:
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3})\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=\diag_{\sharp}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})\mapsto\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})
; here again
\diag_{\sharp}

means that the three distinct eigenvalues are put in the right six locations on the diagonal.

As
m_{V}=n-m_{Q}-m_{\Lambda}=6-3-3=0
, the other three eigenvectors may be chosen
as fixed.
They are found to be
\lambda_{1}=\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}+2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}
with
eigenvector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}
(cf. Eq.(
126
)) and
\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{3}=\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}-\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}
with the two-dimensional eigen-space
spanned by the shear-vectors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{3}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}
, cf. Eqs.(
127
) and (
128
).
The fixed matrix of eigenvectors is thus
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}=[\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{3},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{4},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{5},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{6}]
,
and the six parameters to describe the material are
(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})
.
Finally, the usual 2-step rotation procedure rotates the Kelvin matrix
to the desired coordinate system.

Transversely isotropic material in 3D:

As indicated, these materials have in 3D the symmetry of a circular cylinder.
To simplify the Kelvin matrix by the group
reduction, a rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}))\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
with only
two

rotation axis parameters
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=(q_{1},q_{2})\in\mathcal{Q}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{m_{Q}}
is
required (
m_{Q}=2
, as no rotation around the cylinder axis is necessary)
— such that the cylinder axis aligns with the 3-axis —

[
31
]
with
n-m_{Q}=5
free parameters
(i.e. the total number of parameters is
n=7
) into the form:

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\text{tr-iso-3D}}={\small\begin{bmatrix}\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1133}&0&0&0\\
&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1133}&0&0&0\\
&&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{3333}&0&0&0\\
&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2323}&0&0\\
&\mathrm{SYM}&&&\;\;\;2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2323}&0\\
&&&&&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}-\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}\end{bmatrix}}.

(83)

This Kelvin matrix has generally
[
171
]
four independent eigenvalues resp. Kelvin moduli; and there are two eigenvalues of multiplicity two. One is obviously

\lambda_{4}=\lambda_{5}=2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{2323}
with the fixed two-dimensional eigen-space
spanned by the vectors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{4}=[0,0,0,1,0,0]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{5}=[0,0,0,0,1,0]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
.
The other double eigenvalue is
\lambda_{3}=\lambda_{6}=\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}-\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}
with the fixed two-dimensional eigen-space
spanned by the vectors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{3}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}
(cf. Eq.(
127
)) and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{6}=[0,0,0,0,0,1]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
.
Thus one needs
m_{\Lambda}=4
log-eigenvalue
parameters to generate the diagonal eigenvalue matrix:
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{4})\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=\diag_{\sharp}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})\mapsto\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})
, where again
\diag_{\sharp}

means that the four distinct eigenvalues are put in the proper six locations on the diagonal.

As for tetragonal material above, the other three eigenvectors come
from the diagonalisation of the upper left
3\times 3
block
\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
in
Eq.(
83
), which is identical to that block for tetragonal material.
The eigenvalue
\lambda_{3}
and its eigenvector which can be found by simple
inspection
[
171
]
was already noted above.
The other eigenvectors for the two simple eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
of

\check{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}
depend on an angle-like strain-distribution parameter
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}=[\alpha]
,
and have the same general form
[
171
]
as for tetragonal material Eq.(
81
),
giving
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})
. This finally yields the eigenvector matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})=[\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{3},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{4},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{5},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{6}]
.
Thus a transversely isotropic material including its orientation in the physical space
\mathcal{V}

is completely described by the
n=7
parameters
(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})
, and
the final 2-step rotation procedure rotates the Kelvin matrix to the desired coordinate system.

Isotropic material in 3D:

As is well known, these materials have in 3D the symmetry of a sphere.
No simplification of the Kelvin matrix by the group reduction process is possible, and
no spatial rotations are required (
m_{Q}=0
). The Kelvin matrix has the same form
in any coordinate system with
[
31
]

n=2
free parameters:

\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\text{iso-3D}}={\small\begin{bmatrix}\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}&0&0&0\\
&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}&0&0&0\\
&&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}&0&0&0\\
&&&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}-\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}&0&0\\
&\mathrm{SYM}&&&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}-\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}&0\\
&&&&&\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}-\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}\end{bmatrix}}.

(84)

There is one eigenvalue with multiplicity five,
\lambda_{j}=\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}-\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}=2\,G,(j=2\dots 6)
, with
G
the well-known

shear modulus
, and its fixed five-dimensional eigen-space of shears spanned by

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{y}},\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{3}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{z}}\quad\text{cf. Eqs.(\ref{eq:y-rep3D}) and (\ref{eq:z-rep3D}), and}

(85)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{4}

\displaystyle=[0,0,0,1,0,0]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}},\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{5}=[0,0,0,0,1,0]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}},\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{6}=[0,0,0,0,0,1]^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}.

The simple Kelvin modulus
\lambda_{1}=\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1111}+2\,\mathring{\mathrm{c}}_{1122}=3\,K
,
where
K
is the well-known
bulk modulus
, has the eigenvector

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{1}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{n}}
cf. Eq.(
126
), i.e. volume change.
To generate the diagonal eigenvalue matrix:
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=\diag_{\sharp}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})\mapsto\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})
, where again
\diag_{\sharp}

means that the two distinct eigenvalues are put in the proper six locations on the diagonal.
Thus an isotropic material is completely described by the
n=m_{\Lambda}=2
parameters

(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})
— the log-eigenvalue parameters — to generate the diagonal eigenvalue matrix:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=\diag_{\sharp}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})\mapsto\exp\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{M}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mu}})
. The eigenvector matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}
has the fixed (
m_{V}=0
)
columns
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{v}}_{j},(j=1\dots 6)
, and no final spatial rotation is needed.

4.3
Example

We consider the average orthotropic elastic properties of human cortical femoral bone in 3D, obtained from measurements on 60 specimens, by
[
6
]
. The coefficients are listed in Table
1
.

Young’s

modulus (GPa)

Poisson’s

ratio

Shear

modulus (GPa)

Y_{1}=12

\nu_{21}=0.422
,
\nu_{12}=0.376

G_{12}=4.53

Y_{2}=13.4

\nu_{31}=0.371
,
\nu_{13}=0.222

G_{13}=5.61

Y_{3}=20

\nu_{32}=0.35
,
\nu_{23}=0.235

G_{23}=6.23

Table 1
:
Orthotropic material parameters of cortical femoral bone

Following this, the compliance matrix—the inverse of the elasticity matrix—in Voigt notation is constructed, as shown in Eq.(6) in
[
6
]
. The subsequent inversion of the compliance matrix yields the elasticity matrix. Finally, by multiplying a factor of 2 to the lower-right
3\times 3
block of diagonal elements, we derive the Kelvin matrix of an orthotropic material in a block diagonal form
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\text{ortho-3D}}
, as depicted in Eq.(
75
).

A key objective of this study is to decouple the modelling of scaling, eigen-strain, and orientation parameters in the Kelvin matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}
—see Eq.(
19
) for the spectral decomposition. To represent variations in these aspects, we model a one-dimensional field of the orthotropic elasticity matrices over the spatial domain
x\in[0,1]
.

To this, the SPD matrix at the left end of the domain at
x=0
remains the previously defined
\mathring{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}}_{\text{ortho-3D}}
, which for simplicity is referred to as
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}
. On the other end at
x=1
, the Kelvin matrix denoted as
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{B}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{B}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{B}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{B}}
is defined separately for each variation. For scaling, the eigenvalues
\lambda_{5}
and
\lambda_{6}
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}
are multiplied by a factor of 5. For rotation in physical space, the matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{B}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{B}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{B}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{B}}
is obtained by rotating
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}
by an angle
\theta=60^{\circ}
or
\pi/3
in radians in X-Z plane in clockwise direction i.e, the rotation vector is
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{q}}=(0,\theta,0)
. For eigen-strain variation, the upper-left
3\times 3
block matrix of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}
undergoes a similar rotation, where the rotation-like vector reads
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}=(0,\theta,0)
.

(a)

Riemannian metric for scaling (
\vartheta_{L}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{2})
)

(b)

Euclidean metric (
\vartheta_{2}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{2})
)

Figure 3
:
Visualization of Young’s modulus for the interpolation of Kelvin matrices under varying scaling parameters

The spatial field is built by interpolating from
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}
to
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{B}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{B}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{B}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{B}}
using the Elasticity metric
\tilde{\vartheta}_{E}
as defined in Eq.(
46
) and Appendix
A.2
. Meaning that, for scaling-based interpolation, the metric reduces to log-Euclidean
\vartheta_{L}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{2})
, whereas, for rotations and eigen-strain-based fluctuations, the interpolation is dependent on Riemannian distance for special orthogonal matrices i.e.,
\vartheta_{R}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2})
and
\vartheta_{R}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{2})
, respectively. Additionally, for comparison, we also interpolate the SPD matrices using the usual Euclidean metric
\vartheta_{2}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{2})
, detailed in Appendix
A.1
.

The visualization of interpolation of Kelvin matrices is done by analysing one of the characteristic elastic parameters such as Young’s modulus (
Y
) for a given loading direction, as shown in
[

. Fig.

, while Figs.

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{A}}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{B}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{B}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{B}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{B}}
using both the Riemannian (non-Euclidean) and Euclidean metric.

)

In the case of scaling-based interpolation (Fig.
6(a)
), the determinant of interpolants based on the Euclidean metric
\vartheta_{2}
is consistently higher than that of the log-Euclidean distance
\vartheta_{L}
. On the other hand, in Figs.
6(b)
and
6(c)
for the respective rotation and eigen-strain variations, Euclidean interpolation suffers from the so-called
swelling effect
, wherein the determinant of any interpolant for
0<x<1
exceeds that of the endpoint matrices at
x=0
and
x=1
. Notably, for fixed scaling parameters, the determinant of Riemannian interpolants
\vartheta_{R}
remains unchanged.

For more discussion on this, we refer the reader to
[
89
]
, where the authors make detailed comparisons for second-order diffusion tensors.

5
Conclusion

A novel framework for the probabilistic modelling of fourth-order random material tensor
fields has been introduced, with a focus on an important class of symmetric and positive
definite (SPD) tensors, such as elasticity tensors.

A crucial aspect of material laws governed by such tensors is their invariance under
spatial symmetries. By employing the Kelvin notation and representing elasticity tensors
as Kelvin matrices their essential tensorial properties are preserved, so that the
spatial symmetries and invariances of elasticity tensors through rotations in physical space may
reduce the Kelvin matrix to a block-diagonal form, the so-called group reduction.
Within each group-invariant subspace of this block-diagonal form, the Kelvin matrix
has been further simplified to a diagonal form through spectral decomposition.

The group reduction, combined with spectral decomposition, forms the foundation of
our approach. Based on this context, the stochastic representation has the ability to
separate the modelling of strength (Kelvin moduli), eigen-strain distribution, and spatial
orientation, allowing for independent control of each component. This framework enables
the consideration of random SPD matrices where specific invariance properties are known
for both the entire population and the mean, or where the ensemble exhibits a ‘lower’ class
of invariance compared to the mean. While the stochastic modelling of Kelvin elasticity
matrices adheres to the prior principles established for second-order tensors in

[
160
]
, the inclusion of eigen-strain distributions represents a crucial
extension of these methods to higher-order tensors.

Even-order SPD tensors form an open convex cone within the vector space of physically
symmetric tensors, meaning they constitute a differentiable manifold, but not a vector space.
In the modelling of random tensor fields, it is often preferable to find an unconstrained
representation in a linear vector space. To achieve this, we consider the matrix exponential
and logarithm mappings. Within the proposed probabilistic framework, Lie groups are associated
with diagonal SPD matrices and subgroups of orthogonal matrices, allowing for the independent
modelling of different aspects of the random tensor. The product Lie group representation
is related to the associated Lie algebras for each individual component, the mapping between
them being the component exponential map, which subsequently yields the full representation
of the Kelvin matrix. Furthermore, the Lie representations as
n
-dimensional random fields
of Kelvin matrix parameters across all symmetry classes is outlined, including all eight
elastic symmetry classes in 3D and the four classes in 2D. In summary, the dimension of

n
ranges from 2 to 6 in 2D, and from 2 to 21 in 3D, for isotropic to tri-clinic materials, respectively.

Given the nature of SPD tensors, calculating the average or mean requires special attention.
The conventional Euclidean mean is often not suitable, as it exhibits an undesirable property
known as the swelling effect. Instead, we focus on the more general non-Euclidean Fréchet mean,
which is based on distance measurements other than the Euclidean metric. Accordingly,
we establish criteria for defining an appropriate mean and metric specifically for elasticity.
The term ‘elasticity metric’ or ‘elasticity distance’ is proposed for this metric, which provides
fine control over measuring differences in spatial orientation, eigen-strain distribution, and Kelvin
moduli, allowing for the combination of these factors with adjustable weights. Based on this
distance, one may then formulate the corresponding Fréchet mean, referred to as the
‘elasticity mean’.
To demonstrate this, as an example, we model a one-dimensional spatial field of orthotropic Kelvin matrices using interpolation based on the elasticity metric. The interpolated matrices are visualized by analysing their corresponding Young’s modulus. The results reveal a notable difference in Riemannian and Euclidean metric-based interpolation.

While this research focused on fourth-order SPD tensors, the stochastic modelling approach
outlined here is sufficiently general to be applicable to any even-order physically symmetric
tensors, including those that are also positive definite.

Appendix A
Appendix: Mean and Metric

The parametrisation of the open cone of fourth-order symmetric positive tensors
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})

is connected to the issue on what kind of metric to use on this manifold,
which in turn is connected with what kind of mean or average on should use.
Here we shall merely give a short overview,
for a fuller discussion of these topics, please refer to
[
160
]
and
the references therein, or also
[
139
,
146
,
145
,
117
,
118
,
121
,
48
,
173
,
166
,
50
,
65
,
88
,
64
]
.

A.1
Fréchet Mean

Since antiquity different ways to compute a mean or average have been known,
one just has to look at the classical Pythagorean means. The one which is best known is of
course the arithmetic mean, which is based on the Euclidean metric
\vartheta_{2}
as will be shown.
The other classical means may be obtained as Fréchet means — to be introduced shortly —
with the variational characterisation below in Eq.(
86
)
via different metrics resp. distance functions
\vartheta
.

As noted before, the mean resp. average resp. expected value has a variational
definition, where the metric of the underlying sample space appears naturally.
For the sake of simplicity, consider initially
a set of
n
vectors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}_{1},\dots,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
— obviously the
case
k=1
is the best known — with
\mathbb{R}^{k}
equipped with the standard Euclidean
norm, such that the squared Euclidean distance is

\vartheta_{2}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})^{2}=\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}\right\|_{2}^{2}=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{y}})
. Then the arithmetic mean

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}}^{(2)}
is characterised by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\vartheta_{2}}(\{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\})

\displaystyle:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}}^{(2)}:=\arg\min_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\Psi_{2}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}),\quad\text{ where in this case }

(86)

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\vartheta_{2}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}_{j},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})^{2},

(87)

and
\Psi_{2}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
may be called the
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}
-based variance. The Euler-Lagrange
variational conditions of vanishing derivative of the functional
\Psi_{2}
obviously yield the
usual definition of arithmetic mean. This is easily extended from constant weights
w_{j}≡1/n

to not necessarily equal (probability) weights
w_{j}>0
,
\sum_{j=1}w_{j}=1
,
and any vector space
\mathcal{Y}
equipped with an inner product and corresponding norm.
By taking just two vectors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}_{2}
and weights
w_{1}=\alpha,w_{2}=1-\alpha

with
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
, it is easily gleaned that this furnishes the interpolation
between the two vectors on the shortest path connecting them — a
geodesic
, which in
a Euclidean space is a straight line parallel to
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}_{2}
.

Going a step further, for a — possibly continuous — probability distribution
\mathbb{P}

of a
\mathcal{Y}
-valued random variable
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{y}}(\omega)
defined on a probability space

(\Omega,\mathbb{P})
with
\mathcal{Y}
some normed vector space, the
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}
-based variance is defined as

\Psi_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})=\int_{\Omega}\vartheta_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{y}}(\omega),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})^{2}\,\mathbb{P}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega)\quad\text{ with }\quad\vartheta_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{y}}(\omega),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}):=\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{y}}(\omega)-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\right\|_{\mathcal{Y}},

(88)

and the variational characterisation Eq.(
86
