) is still used to define
the arithmetic mean
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}}^{(\mathbb{P})}
or
\mathbb{P}
-expected value
\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbb{P}}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{y}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{y}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{y}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{y}})=\arg\min_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}}\Psi_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
.
Observe that all that was needed is a metric
space
\mathcal{Y}
with a metric or distance function
\vartheta_{\mathcal{Y}}
. Such a mean defined
analogous to Eq.(
86
) on a metric space with a general
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}
-based variance

\Psi_{\vartheta}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}})
as in Eq.(
88
) with a general metric
\vartheta

is called a
Fréchet
mean
[
139
,
146
,
145
,
173
,
166
,
50
]
.

On the manifold of positive definite tensors
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}\subset\mathfrak{sym}
, when using the Euclidean metric
and the corresponding arithmetic average resp. mean derived from it, one observes

[
146
,
2
,
116
,
4
,
5
,
43
,
44
,
184
,
54
,
48
]

undesirable
swelling, fattening, and shrinking
effects in interpolation
resp. averaging
[
157
,
89
,
158
,
62
,
61
]
.
These undesirable effects will affect the corresponding mean in a similar manner.
This, as well as the wish to satisfy the desiderata for a mean formulated in
Section
3.3
, which are not satisfied by the arithmetic mean, but are
easily seen to be inherited from the corresponding metric, is the reason to look at other
distance functions on the cone of positive definite tensors. In the following
an ‘elasticity distance’ on
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
will be defined via an ‘elasticity metric’ on

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}=\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\times\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})\times\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
, which in turn can
be used to construct a corresponding ‘elasticity Fréchet mean’ analogous to
Eqs.(
86
) and (
88
).

A.2
Elasticity Distance

The manifold of positive definite tensors
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}\subset\mathfrak{sym}
is geometrically an open cone in
the vector space of symmetric tensors
\mathfrak{sym}
, and may be equipped with a metric in different ways,
cf.
[
92
,
33
,
146
,
2
,
116
,
117
,
4
,
5
,
43
,
44
,
54
,
48
,
122
]
, as well as

[
4
,
5
]
and
[
89
,
158
,
62
,
61
,
160
]
.
In these non-Euclidean metrics mentioned, the convex cone
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}
is metrically complete.
These metrics also greatly reduce the swelling, fattening, and shrinking effects alluded to above.

As the approach in this work is to represent a positive definite tensor as

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}
with an
orthogonal
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}})\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
induced by a spatial rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
,
a strain resp. stress distribution
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
and the Kelvin moduli resp. stiffnesses
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\in\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
,
the representation is characterised by the triple
(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}})
from
the product manifold / product Lie group

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}=\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})\times\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})\times\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
.
Each of the factors of the product
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}
being a finite dimensional
Lie group under matrix multiplication,
the focus here is on how to endow such a locally compact Lie group with a Riemannian metric
and derive a metric structure from it.

Two instances when this can be done is when the Lie group is compact or when it is Abelian

[
159
,
41
,
92
,
167
,
87
,
1
]
,
as this allows the construction of a bi-invariant metric. Observe that the whole
manifold resp. open cone
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})

is
not
a Lie group under normal matrix multiplication, but it can be endowed with an
Abelian product
[
4
,
5
]
,
which turns it into a Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sym}(\mathcal{T})
, and
which for commuting elements of
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
is the same as the usual matrix product.
But for the product Lie group
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}
note that
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
and

\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
are compact Lie groups, and
\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
is a non-compact but Abelian Lie group.
As the focus in this work is to separate the effects of spatial orientation
as represented by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
, strain resp. stress distribution as
represented by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
, and stiffnesses as represented by

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
, we shall not follow directly

[
4
,
5
]
and use the above
mentioned Abelian Lie group structure on
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
, as their approach tends
to ignore the possible non-commutativity of elements of
\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})

and mixes orientation, stress distribution, and stiffness in the so-called
log-Euclidean

metric. Here we rather follow
[
184
,
89
,
158
,
62
,
61
,
160
]
and
look for a metric on the product Lie group

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}=\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})\times\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})\times\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
.

To give a bit of background for the construction of the elasticity metric to be introduced below,
the general situation
[
41
,
92
,
167
,
87
,
1
]

is such that in a Lie group
G
,
viewed as a differentiable manifold, a neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}(g)\subset G
and tangent space

\mathrm{T}_{g}G
at each point
g\in G
can easily be mapped to the identity element
e\in G
via
the group operation, e.g.
g^{-1}\mathcal{U}(g)=\{g^{-1}h\mid h\in\mathcal{U}(g)\}
is a neighbourhood
of
e\in G
, and the tangent spaces
\mathrm{T}_{g}G
and
\mathrm{T}_{e}G
are isomorphic.
The tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{e}G
at the identity
e\in G
is equivalent to the Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}
associated to
G
. For the compact or Abelian Lie groups, any inner
product on the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
gives rise to a Riemannian structure on the whole
manifold resp. Lie group
G
, which allows one to measure distances on the group and
Riemannian manifold
G
at least locally.
The mapping from the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
to the Lie group
G

is given by the exponential map
\exp:\mathfrak{g}\to G
of Lie group theory,
which has a (generally only locally around
e\in G
)
inverse
\log:G\to\mathfrak{g}
. This in turn allows one to define geodesics as
curves of locally minimal length,
and hence the distance between points on the manifold as the minimal length
of the geodesics joining them, cf.
[
41
,
92
,
167
,
87
,
1
]
, giving the manifold the structure of a complete
metric space. In the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, which is a Euclidean vector space, geodesics
through the origin are straight lines, and these are mapped by the exponential map to
geodesics on the Lie group
G
through the identity element
e\in G
, as

\mathfrak{g}\ni 0\mapsto\exp(0)=e\in G
. From the identity
e\in G
the geodesics can
be mapped to the neighbourhood of any other element
g\in G
by the group operation.

On the compact Lie group of proper rotations of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the special orthogonal group

\mathrm{SO}(n)
with the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(n)
of skew symmetric matrices, this construction
gives the distance between two elements
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}\in\mathrm{SO}(n)
as
[
92
,
115
]
:

\vartheta_{R}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}):=\left\|\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}^{-1})\right\|_{F}=\left\|\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}})\right\|_{F},

(89)

where
\left\|\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}\right\|_{F}=\sqrt{\tr\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}}
is the Frobenius norm. Note that
by the group property
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}\in\mathrm{SO}(n)
, so that

\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{W}}\in\mathfrak{so}(n)
is a skew symmetric matrix. This
yields a distance for the factors
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
resp.
\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
and
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})

of the representation product Lie group
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}
.

Diagonal positive definite matrices
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\in\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(n)
form an
Abelian or commutative Lie group
under matrix multiplication, with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{diag}(n)\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the diagonal
matrices. As
\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(n)
is Abelian resp. commutative, one can now freely use the
Log-Euclidean norm of
[
4
,
5
]

as a distance measure on
\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(n)
.
For two elements
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{2}\in\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(n)
this is given by

\vartheta_{L}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{2}):=\left\|\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{2}^{-1})\right\|_{F}=\left\|\log\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{1}-\log\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{2}\right\|_{F}.

(90)

By the group property
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{2}^{-1}\in\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(n)
, so that

\log(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{2}^{-1})\in\mathfrak{diag}(n)
is a diagonal matrix.
Thus this yields a distance for the diagonal component
\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})

in the representation product Lie group
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}
.

The product Lie group
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}=\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})\times\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})\times\mathrm{Diag^{+}}(\mathcal{T})
has as
identity element three copies of the appropriate identity matrices
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{e}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{e}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{e}}:=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}_{\mathcal{V}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}_{\mathcal{T}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}_{\mathcal{T}})
, and
as Lie algebra the direct sum of the individual Lie algebras with a product exponential map:

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfit{e}}{\textstyle\mathsfit{e}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfit{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfit{e}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}

\displaystyle=:\mathfrak{s}_{p}:=\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{V})\oplus\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})\oplus\mathfrak{diag}(\mathcal{T}),\quad\text{ with }

(91)

\displaystyle\exp_{\mathfrak{s}}:\,\mathfrak{s}

\displaystyle\ni(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{D}})\mapsto(\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}),\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}),\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{D}}))=(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}})\in\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}.

(92)

By the construction given above, for each factor of the product Lie group
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}

one has a bi-invariant metric, and
we follow and extend
[
184
,
89
,
62
,
61
,
160
]
, and define a
Riemannian product metric on the product Lie group, here termed an ‘elasticity metric’.
For two elements
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{2}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
with representations

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{j}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{j}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{j}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{j}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{j}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{j}
,
j=1,2
,
the squared product distance
\tilde{\vartheta}_{E}^{2}
is given by

\tilde{\vartheta}_{E}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{2})^{2}=\vartheta_{L}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{2})^{2}+c_{\mathcal{V}}\vartheta_{R}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2})^{2}+c_{\mathcal{T}}\vartheta_{R}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{2})^{2},

(93)

with two positive constants
c_{\mathcal{V}},c_{\mathcal{T}}
, which allow one to tune the sensitivity
of the product distance
\tilde{\vartheta}_{E}
to the different components in the product Lie group
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}
.

As the representation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}
of a
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})

on
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}
is not unique
[
48
,
62
,
63
]
,
one minimises
[
88
,
64
]
over all possible representations to give the ‘elasticity distance’
\vartheta_{E}
:

\hat{\vartheta}_{E}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{2})=\min\{\tilde{\vartheta}_{E}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{1}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{1}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{2}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{2}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{2}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{2}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{2})=\tilde{\vartheta}_{E}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{2})\mid\\
(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{j},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{j},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{j})\in\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{j}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{j}),\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{j}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{j}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{j}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}_{j}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}_{j}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}_{j};\;j=1,2\}.

(94)

Unfortunately, this is not yet a metric — it does not satisfy the triangle inequality,
see the discussion in
[
48
]
—
but can be made into one by recognising that the triples
(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}})\in\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}

which construct the same
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\Lambda}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{V}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}
form an
equivalence class in
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{S}}{\textstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{S}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{S}}_{p}
, and we are really looking for a metric on the quotient space.
The proper construction for a metric on a quotient of a metric space is

\vartheta_{E}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{a},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{b})=\inf\{\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\hat{\vartheta}_{E}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{j},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{j+1})\mid n\geq 2,\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{1}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{a},\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{n}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{b}\;\\
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{j}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T}),j=1,\dots,n\},

(95)

i.e. by allowing ‘hops’
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{j}
between
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{a}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{b}
.
Both expressions Eqs.(
94
) and (
95
) are not easy to compute,
so that often one takes just
\tilde{\vartheta}_{E}
from Eq.(
93
), which
is a Riemannian metric, and should be good enough when the differences between

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{1}
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{2}
are not large.

With the elasticity metric
\vartheta_{E}
one may then define an elasticity Fréchet mean
or expected value
\mathbb{E}_{\vartheta_{E}}
for a random elasticity tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{r}(\omega)
,
defined as a random variable
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{r}:\Omega\to\mathrm{Sym}^{+}(\mathcal{T})
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\vartheta_{E}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{r})

\displaystyle:=\arg\min_{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{+}}\Psi_{\vartheta_{E}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}),\quad\text{ with}

(96)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\vartheta_{E}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Omega}\vartheta_{E}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{C}}_{r}(\omega),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}})^{2}\,\mathbb{P}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega).

(97)

It is not difficult to see that the elasticity Fréchet mean
\mathbb{E}_{\vartheta_{E}}

in Eq.(
96
) will satisfy all the desiderata for a mean formulated in
Section
3.3
in case the
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}
-based variance
\Psi_{\vartheta_{E}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\bar{C}}})

in Eq.(
97
) satisfies these. But this follows from the fact that the elasticity metric

\vartheta_{E}
in Eq.(
94
) satisfies the requirements for a metric in Section
3.3
,
which in turn follows from the satisfaction of these requirements by the product distance

\tilde{\vartheta}_{E}
in Eq.(
93
).

Appendix B
Appendix: Rotations

A proper rotation is an orthogonal tensor
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(n)\subset\mathscr{L}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
i.e.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}
(with the identity

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}=(\updelta_{ik})\in\mathrm{SO}(n)
and the Kronecker-
\updelta
), and hence

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}^{-1}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
, with
\det\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}=1
.
These proper orthogonal matrices form the special orthogonal Lie group
\mathrm{SO}(n)
,
a differentiable manifold with a differentiable group operation,
cf.
[
92
,
115
,
20
]
.
This group is a connected subgroup of the group of all rotations
\mathrm{SO}(n)\subset\mathrm{O}(n)
,
and contains the identity,
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}\in\mathrm{SO}(n)
.

B.1
Lie algebra and exponential map

The Lie algebra — the tangent space at the identity — of

\mathrm{SO}(n)
is denoted by
\mathfrak{so}(n)
and is the vector space of
skew-symmetric

real matrices, i.e.
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}=-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}
,
[
92
,
115
,
20
]
.
Like in any such Lie algebra, there is an exponential map
\exp:\mathfrak{so}(n)\to\mathrm{SO}(n)
.
Here, this is the common matrix exponential,
and in the case of
\mathrm{SO}(n)
it is surjective, hence for any
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(n)
there is
a
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}\in\mathfrak{so}(n)
such that

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}=\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}),

(98)

a representation which will be used in the sequel, as it allows one to work in the
vector space
\mathfrak{so}(n)
. The
\log:\mathrm{SO}(n)\to\mathfrak{so}(n)
can be defined as the
main branch of the inverse of Eq.(
98
), defined on a neighbourhood of
the identity
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}\in\mathrm{SO}(n)
. For the parametrisation of
\mathfrak{so}(n)
, a linear bijective map

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}:\mathbb{R}^{\dim\mathfrak{so}(n)}\to\mathfrak{so}(n)
will be defined, which by slight abuse of
notation will always be denoted by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}
, regardless of the dimension
n
.

It may be of interest to recall that
the matrix Lie algebras (like
\mathfrak{so}(n)
) have as Lie product the matrix commutator

[\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}_{2}]=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}_{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}_{2}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}_{2}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}_{1}.

(99)

If one defines the group commutator on
\mathrm{SO}(n)
as

[\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}]_{\mathrm{SO}}:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}^{-1}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}^{-1},

(100)

then it holds with — using Eq. (
98
) —
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}=\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}_{1})
and

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}=\exp(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}_{2})
, that

[\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}_{2}]_{\mathrm{SO}}=\exp([\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}_{2}]).

(101)

B.2
Rotations in
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
in 3D and 2D

In 3D with
n=d=3
,
\dim\mathfrak{so}(3)=3
, so
\mathfrak{so}(3)\cong\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which is a special
case. For a vector

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}=(p_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the corresponding skew matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}\in\mathfrak{so}(3)
is

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}):=\begin{bmatrix}\phantom{-}0&-p_{3}&\phantom{-}p_{2}\\
\phantom{-}p_{3}&\phantom{-}0&-p_{1}\\
-p_{2}&\phantom{-}p_{1}&\phantom{-}0\end{bmatrix},\quad\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}=-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}^{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{T}}{\textstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{T}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{T}}}.

(102)

It is sometimes convenient to write
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}=\theta\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}
with a unit vector (
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}\cdot\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}=1
),
so that
|\theta|
is the length of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}
. Here

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}})\;\text{ is used, s.t. }\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}=\theta\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}.

(103)

If
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is equipped with the usual vector cross
product, it becomes a Lie algebra, one has for any
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
that

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}\times\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{x}}
with
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}})
,
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}
becomes a Lie algebra isomorphism

[\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}_{2}]=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}\times\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}),\;\text{ where }\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}_{j}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}_{j}),\,j=1,2.

According to Euler’s theorem, a proper rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(3)
with rotation angle

\theta
has a normalised eigenvector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with unit eigenvalue, the

rotation axis
, such that
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}
— and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{0}}
, where

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}=\log\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}
— and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}
may be represented via the Rodrigues formula

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}=\exp(\theta\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}+(\sin\theta)\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}+(1-\cos\theta)\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}^{2},\;\text{ where }\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}).

(104)

In 2D with
n=d=2
one has
\dim\mathfrak{so}(2)=1
, hence the Lie algebra

\mathfrak{so}(2)\cong\mathbb{R}
of 2D skew matrices and the
Lie group
\mathrm{SO}(2)
are one dimensional.
This is equivalent to a 3D rotation around, say, the 3-axis,
and can easily be seen by only looking at a typical rotation with angle
\theta

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}=\begin{bmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\phantom{-}\cos\theta\end{bmatrix}.

(105)

To write in 2D the equivalent of Eq. (
104
), a
convenient basis in
\mathfrak{so}(2)
is

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}=\begin{bmatrix}0&-1\\
1&\phantom{-}0\end{bmatrix},

(106)

and all matrices in
\mathfrak{so}(2)
are scalar multiples of it, so that

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}=\exp(\theta\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}})=(\cos\theta)\,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{I}}+(\sin\theta)\,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}=\begin{bmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\phantom{-}\cos\theta\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{SO}(2).

(107)

Observe that any
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{P}}\in\mathfrak{so}(n)
has purely imaginary or vanishing eigenvalues, and the
exponential map has period
2\uppi
parallel to the imaginary axis, hence this is inherited
by the Rodrigues formulas Eqs.(
104
) and (
107
), as can also be seen by
the explicit expressions in terms of trigonometric function of the angle
\theta
.
Obviously a rotation of
\theta=\uppi
about some axis is the same as a rotation
of
\theta=-\uppi
. Hence those maps are only unique within a ball of radius
\uppi

for the vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
in Eq.(
102
), resp. the interval
]-\uppi,\uppi]

for the angle
\theta
in 2D in Eq.(
107
).

B.3
Rotations in
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})

What will be needed beyond the 2- and 3D cases just described is the modelling of
rotations in the strain-space
\mathcal{T}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{k}.
The maximum number of dimensions here
is
\dim\mathcal{T}=k=6
for the 3D case, but occasionally only a subspace is needed.
The special orthogonal group
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
has dimension of its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})
,
the space of skew symmetric matrices. As
\dim\mathfrak{so}(\mathbb{R}^{k})=k(k-1)/2
, one obtains here
in the case
\mathcal{T}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{k}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{6}
that
\dim\mathfrak{so}(\mathbb{R}^{6})=15
. We shall describe
the mapping
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}:\mathbb{R}^{\dim\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})}\to\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})
,
already given for the 2- and 3D cases in the previous Section
B.2
.

Denote the canonical unit vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{15}
by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{1},\dots,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{15}
, and a general vector

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}=\sum_{j=1}^{15}p_{j}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{15}
. Then we define the mapping
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}
,
which maps
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}
to the skew-symmetric matrix
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}\in\mathfrak{so}(6)
, and which is completely defined
by its strictly upper triangle, via

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}):=\begin{bmatrix}0&p_{15}&p_{10}&p_{6}&p_{3}&p_{1}\\
\cdot&\phantom{0}0&p_{14}&p_{9}&p_{5}&p_{2}\\
\cdot&\phantom{0}\cdot&\phantom{0}0&p_{13}&p_{8}&p_{4}\\
\cdot&\phantom{0}\cdot&\phantom{0}\cdot&\phantom{0}0&p_{12}&p_{7}\\
\cdot&\phantom{0}\cdot&\phantom{0}\cdot&\phantom{0}\cdot&\phantom{0}0&p_{11}\\
\cdot&\phantom{0}\cdot&\phantom{0}\cdot&\phantom{0}\cdot&\phantom{0}\cdot&\phantom{0}0\end{bmatrix},

(108)

i.e. by the 5 upper diagonals,
and where the strictly lower triangle has to be filled-in anti-symmetrically. Again,
by slight abuse of notation, we continue to designate this map by
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}
. In case the
map is used for dimensions
3<k<6
, one may just use the strict upper triangle of
the
k
upper diagonals, set the main diagonal to zero,
and complete the square matrix anti-symmetrically. It may be noted that for dimensions

k=2,3
this deviates from what was described in Eqs.(
102
) and (
106
) by
a permutation of indices resp. sign. This is due to the fact that in 3D one wants the
parameter vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{p}}
to be the eigenvector resp. rotation axis. For these cases
k=2,3

we shall stay with the convention as in Eqs.(
102
) and (
106
), and only
use Eq.(
108
) for
k>3
.

\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
-induced rotations in
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
:

As already described in Section
2.1
, a rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})
of the
coordinate system in
\mathcal{V}
induces a transformation of
the stress and strain tensors as in Eq.(
4
)

[
113
,
114
]
. This is an element

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}})\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
, given by an injective group homomorphism

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}:\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V})\to\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
.
A similar correspondence exists on the corresponding Lie algebras:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}})\in\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})
, given by a map

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}:\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{V})\to\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T})
.

Rotations induced from 3D, i.e.
d=3
,
k=6
:

In the 3D (
\mathcal{V}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\mathcal{T}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{6}
) case of the Kelvin representation
in
\mathcal{T}
, the rotation
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}
is represented
[
113
,
114
]

by an orthogonal matrix

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}})\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathcal{T}^{\otimes 2}=\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{T})
,
so that Eq.(
4
) becomes
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{s}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{s}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{s}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{s}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}
and

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{e}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{e}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{e}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{e}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{e}}
, where in terms of the components
(Q_{ik})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(3)
in 3D
the map
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}:\mathrm{SO}(3)\to\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
is

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}):=\\
{\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}Q_{11}^{2}&Q_{12}^{2}&Q_{13}^{2}&\sqrt{2}Q_{12}Q_{13}&\sqrt{2}Q_{11}Q_{13}&\sqrt{2}Q_{11}Q_{12}\\
Q_{21}^{2}&Q_{22}^{2}&Q_{23}^{2}&\sqrt{2}Q_{22}Q_{23}&\sqrt{2}Q_{21}Q_{23}&\sqrt{2}Q_{22}Q_{21}\\
Q_{31}^{2}&Q_{32}^{2}&Q_{33}^{2}&\sqrt{2}Q_{33}Q_{32}&\sqrt{2}Q_{33}Q_{31}&\sqrt{2}Q_{31}Q_{32}\\
\sqrt{2}Q_{21}Q_{31}&\sqrt{2}Q_{22}Q_{32}&\sqrt{2}Q_{23}Q_{33}&Q_{22}Q_{33}+Q_{23}Q_{32}&Q_{21}Q_{33}+Q_{23}Q_{31}&Q_{21}Q_{32}+Q_{22}Q_{31}\\
\sqrt{2}Q_{11}Q_{31}&\sqrt{2}Q_{12}Q_{32}&\sqrt{2}Q_{13}Q_{33}&Q_{12}Q_{33}+Q_{13}Q_{32}&Q_{11}Q_{33}+Q_{13}Q_{31}&Q_{11}Q_{32}+Q_{12}Q_{31}\\
\sqrt{2}Q_{11}Q_{21}&\sqrt{2}Q_{12}Q_{22}&\sqrt{2}Q_{13}Q_{23}&Q_{12}Q_{23}+Q_{13}Q_{22}&Q_{11}Q_{23}+Q_{13}Q_{21}&Q_{11}Q_{22}+Q_{12}Q_{21}\end{bmatrix}.}

(109)

To translate Eq.(
104
), where
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}=\exp(\theta\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}})
and
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}})
is
formed from the normalised rotation axis vector
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}=(r_{j})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
as in Eq.(
102
),
one may define a map
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}:\mathfrak{so}(3)\to\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathfrak{so}(6)
(and combine it with

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathfrak{so}(3)
in Eq.(
102
))

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{R}}):={\scriptsize\begin{bmatrix}0&0&0&0&\phantom{-}\sqrt{2}\,r_{2}&-\sqrt{2}\,r_{3}\\
0&0&0&-\sqrt{2}\,r_{1}&0&\phantom{-}\sqrt{2}\,r_{3}\\
0&0&0&\phantom{-}\sqrt{2}\,r_{1}&-\sqrt{2}\,r_{2}&0\\
0&\phantom{-}\sqrt{2}\,r_{1}&-\sqrt{2}\,r_{1}&0&\phantom{-}r_{3}&-r_{2}\\
-\sqrt{2}\,r_{2}&0&\phantom{-}\sqrt{2}\,r_{2}&-r_{3}&0&\phantom{-}r_{1}\\
\phantom{-}\sqrt{2}\,r_{3}&-\sqrt{2}\,r_{3}&0&\phantom{-}r_{2}&-r_{1}&0\end{bmatrix}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{skw}}{\textstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{skw}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{r}})).

(110)

As the Lie group
\mathrm{SO}(3)
and its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(3)
are three-dimensional,
the image
\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T}):=\im\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}\subset\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
of
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}
is a three-dimensional
Lie subgroup
\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})
of the full 15-dimensional Lie group
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})

of proper rotations on
\mathcal{T}
, and a similar relation holds for the Lie algebras:

\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T}):=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{V}))\subset\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T}),\quad\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T}):=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}(\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{V}))\subset\mathfrak{so}(\mathcal{T}).

(111)

It may be gleaned from Eq.(
110
), using Eq.(
108
),
that the canonical unit vectors
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
are thus represented in
\mathbb{R}^{15}
by

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{1})=-\sqrt{2}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{9}+\sqrt{2}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{13}+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{11};

(112)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})=\phantom{-}\sqrt{2}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{3}-\sqrt{2}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{8\phantom{3}}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{7},\text{ and }

(113)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{3})=-\sqrt{2}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}+\sqrt{2}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{2\phantom{3}}+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{12},

(114)

by which one extends the map
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{trep}}:\mathfrak{so}(3)\to\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathfrak{so}(6)

Thus, the three vectors

),(

The Rodrigues formula Eq.(

) then becomes
[

,

:

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\textstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Trep}}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}})=\exp(\theta\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}+(\sin\theta)\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}+(1-\cos\theta)\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}^{2}\\
+\frac{1}{3}(\sin\theta)\;(1-\cos\theta)(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}^{3})+\frac{1}{6}(1-\cos\theta)^{2}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}^{2}+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}^{4}),

.

It is easy to see that the orthogonal complement
\mathcal{Q}^{\perp}\subset\mathbb{R}^{15}

— orthogonal w.r.t. the canonical inner product in

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{4};\quad\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}_{2}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{5};\quad\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}_{3}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{6};\quad\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}_{4}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{10};\quad\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}_{5}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{14};\quad\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}_{6}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{e}}_{15};

(116)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}_{11}

\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})

(p_{1},\dots,p_{12})

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{P}}

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{s}}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{s}}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{s}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{\tilde{s}}}=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{s}}

\phantom{-}\sqrt{2}\;\sin\theta\cos\theta&-\sqrt{2}\;\sin\theta\cos\theta&\cos^{2}\theta-\sin^{2}\theta\end{bmatrix}}\\
={\small\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}1+\cos 2\theta&1-\cos 2\theta&-\sqrt{2}\;\sin 2\theta\\

\phantom{-}\sqrt{2}\;\sin 2\theta&-\sqrt{2}\;\sin 2\theta&\hskip 9.24994pt\;\;\,2\;\cos 2\theta\end{bmatrix}}\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T}).

(117)

Just as in Eq.(
115
), this also can be written in the form of an exponential

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\exp(\theta\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}})=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{I}}+\frac{\sin(2\theta)}{2}\;\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}-\frac{1+\cos(2\theta)}{4}\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}^{2}\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T}),

where from Eq.(

0&0&\phantom{-}\sqrt{2}\\
\phantom{-}\sqrt{2}&-\sqrt{2}&0\end{bmatrix}\in\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})

(119)

is a constant matrix. This is a reflection of the fact that
the matrices
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}\in\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
of the form Eq. (

),

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{Q}}\in\mathrm{SO}(2)

are only
a one-dimensional Lie subgroup

of

-induced rotations of

\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
. And as

and

are one-dimensional, hence so are their images
\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})

in Eq.(

As

, the parameter space is
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with the canonical basis

\{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{1},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{2},\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{3}\}
. One may observe with the help of Eq.(

) that

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}:=-\sqrt{2}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}+\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{2})
is mapped onto

in Eq.(
119
), and thus

\spn\{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}\}=\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the subspace that describes spatial rotations,
such that with a rotation angle
\theta
one has with Eq.(
104
)

\mathcal{Q}\to\mathfrak{st}(\mathcal{T})\to\mathrm{ST}(\mathcal{T})\subset\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T});\quad q:=\theta\mapsto q\,\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}\mapsto\theta\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}\mapsto\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{Q}}=\exp(\theta\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{R}}).

(120)

Obviously, not every
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\textstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsfbfit{U}}\in\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
is of that spatially induced form, and in the parameter
space one may take the orthogonal complement
\mathcal{Q}^{\perp}
of

with basis

\mathcal{Q}^{\perp}=\spn\{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}_{1}:=\sqrt{2}(\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}-\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}),\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{k}}_{2}:=\mathchoice{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\textstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}{\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{g}}_{3}\}.

(121)

A not spatially induced rotation in
\mathrm{SO}(\mathcal{T})
is thus described by two parameters

(s_{1},s_{2})
, which lead with the use of Eqs.(
102
) and (
